## Page 1


QUADRATIC SPACES AND ORTHOGONAL GROUPS
OVER SEMILOCAL RINGS
PHILIPPE GILLE AND ERHARD NEHER
Abstract: We prove Springer’s Odd Degree Theorem for quadratic forms
over LG rings, and Scharlau’s and Knebusch’s norm principles for quadratic
forms over semilocal rings. We present applications to the flat cohomology
of spin groups and ´etale norm groups.
MSC: 11E08, 11E81, 14L15, 14L30, 14M15, 19G12.

Introduction
The algebraic theory of quadratic forms, initiated by Ernst Witt’s founda-
tional 1937 paper, has evolved into a vast and intricate domain intersecting
algebra, geometry, and topology. Traditionally, this theory was developed
over fields, where the structural behavior of quadratic spaces and their as-
sociated orthogonal groups admits a high degree of tractability. Over fields,
the classification of forms, the structure of orthogonal groups, and the be-
havior of quadrics under field extensions follow well-established paradigms.
However, the progression of mathematics necessitates extending these con-
cepts to more general bases, replacing fields by arbitrary commutative rings.
The transition from fields to rings introduces profound algebraic and geomet-
ric challenges. Properties that are trivial or vacuous over fields -—- such as
the distinction between regular and nonsingular forms, or the non-freeness of
projective modules –— become important challenges in the broader context.
These lecture notes provide a rigorous and detailed exposition of the the-
ory of quadratic forms and orthogonal group schemes over general base rings,
with a particular emphasis on semilocal rings and, more generally, rings sat-
isfying the Local-Global (LG) condition. A ring R is an LG ring if every
polynomial which represents a unit locally at each maximal ideal also repre-
sents a unit globally. The LG condition, a property abstracting key features
of semilocal rings, proves to be a powerful framework for extending classi-
cal theorems from base fields to base rings. This condition guarantees, for
instance, that quadratic spaces have Witt cancellation, 4.17, and allow a
decomposition into rank 2 subspaces, 4.20, thus facilitating inductive argu-
ments that would otherwise fail outside the realm of fields.
The central narrative of these notes weaves together the algebraic struc-
ture of the orthogonal group schemes O(q) and its special orthogonal sub-
group SO(q), the geometric action of these groups on the associated spheres
and quadrics, and the deep constraints imposed by the norm principles of
Knebusch and Scharlau under ´etale extensions. By systematically exploring
these areas, we aim to bridge the gap between classical quadratic form the-
ory over fields and the modern schematic approach, highlighting both the
direct generalizations and the subtle divergences that arise over rings. We
now describe the main advances of these notes in more detail.
Orthogonal Groups and their Actions on Spheres and Quadrics, §6–§8. A
fundamental problem in the study of quadratic spaces over rings concerns
the structure and behavior of the orthogonal group scheme O(q) and its
subgroups. Over a field F, the generation of the orthogonal group O(q) =
O(q)(F) by reflections (the Cartan-Dieudonn´e-Kneser Theorem 5.2) and the


## Page 3


QUADRATIC SPACES OVER SEMILOCAL RINGS
3
transitivity of its action on spheres of given length, a consequence of Witt’s
Extension Theorem, are cornerstones of the theory. When the base ring is
local or semilocal, these classical theorems require careful reformulation and
the introduction of new geometric tools.
We begin by proving surjectivity of the canonical map SO(q)(R) →
SO(q)(R/a) for a nonsingular q and any ideal a of the semilocal ring R
contained in the Jacobson radical of R, 7.1. The proof relies on the smooth-
ness of the group scheme SO(q), demonstrating the immediate utility of the
language of group schemes. An immediate consequence is the surjectivity of
O(q)(R) →O(q)(R/a) for local rings (R, m) when the quadratic form q is
regular or when a = m. This surjectivity is not merely a technical lemma; it
is a vital lifting property that allows one to deduce global structural features
of O(q) and SO(q) from their behavior over residue fields. However, this lift-
ing fails when q is not regular and a ⊊m, highlighting the subtle pathologies
present over rings of characteristic 2 where the difference between regular
and nonsingular forms becomes important.
Moving beyond the groups O(q) and SO(q), we deeply analyze their ac-
tions on spheres and quadrics. Let Ssm
q,a denote the scheme of smooth points
of the sphere Sq,a with R–points {x ∈M : q(x) = a}. Extending Witt’s
transitivity in the field case, we prove that for a quadratic space (M, q) of
rank at least 3 over a semilocal ring R, the group SO(q)(R) acts transitively
on Ssm
q,a(R) for any unit a ∈R×, 7.5. This transitivity is achieved by first
verifying it over the residue fields (using Witt’s theorem) and then lifting
the isometries via the aforementioned surjectivity properties. This allows us
to prove that, under mild conditions on the residue fields, SO(q)(R) can be
used to construct regular planes and split off hyperbolic direct summands,
mimicking the classical orthogonal decomposition process, 7.8 and 7.9.
Furthermore, we explore the action of O(q) and SO(q) on higher-rank
quadrics, i.e., the scheme Qν(q) parameterizing totally isotropic, comple-
mented submodules of rank ν. These higher-rank quadrics are shown to
be smooth projective schemes exactly when the quadratic form is nonsin-
gular, 8.3. A central geometric result of these notes is the identification
of the stabilizers of this action. We demonstrate that the stabilizer of an
R-point in Qν(q) is a parabolic subgroup P of the reductive group scheme
SO(q). Moreover, when the rank ν is not maximal, i.e., Qν(q) is not a La-
grangian, the quadric Qν(q) is isomorphic to the quotient space SO(q)/P,
8.4. This provides a complete translation between the geometric objects
(quadrics) and the structural components of the algebraic group (parabolic
subgroups).
The case of maximal totally isotropic submodules — the Lagrangian
quadric L(q) — requires a more nuanced treatment. Here, the O(q) ac-
tion is transitive, but no longer that of SO(q). To understand the geometry
of L(q), we explicitly construct its Stein factorization, a result of Deligne
for quadratic forms over base schemes. We detail Deligne’s result showing
that the structural morphism L(q) →Spec(R) factors through the spectrum

Springer’s Theorem for LG Rings, §9. One of the most celebrated the-
orems in the classical theory of quadratic forms is Springer’s Odd Degree
Theorem (1952). It asserts that if a quadratic form over a field F (of char-
acteristic ̸= 2) becomes isotropic over a finite field extension K/F of odd
degree, then the form must already be isotropic over the base field F. This
result imposes severe constraints on the anisotropic behavior of quadratic
forms under field extensions and is a fundamental tool in the algebraic theory
of quadratic forms.
A major objective of these notes is to extend Springer’s Theorem to the
far broader context of LG rings: Let (M, q) be a quadratic space over an
LG ring R, and let S be a finite, locally free R-algebra of constant odd
rank. Under suitable conditions (for instance, if S is generated by a single
element, or if S is ´etale and R satisfies certain primitive criteria), if the
extended quadratic space (M, q)S is isotropic over S, then the original space
(M, q) is already isotropic over R, 9.1, 9.2.
Our proof of this generalization is highly non-trivial and departs signif-
icantly from the classical field-theoretic arguments.
Instead, we employ
a geometric approach centered on the construction and representability of
what we term “Springer functors”, 9.12. For a one-generated odd-degree
extension S = R[x], the Springer functor parameterizes specific isotropic
vectors in the extended space (M, q)S that satisfy certain conditions relative
to the minimal polynomial of x. We prove that this functor is representable
by a smooth, quasi-compact open subscheme of an affine space, 9.14.
By establishing that this representing scheme has geometrically integral
fibers and is non-empty over LG rings (provided the rank of the space is
at least 3), we guarantee the existence of rational points. These rational
points correspond to specific isotropic vectors that allow us to construct an
intermediate algebra of strictly lower odd degree over which the form is also
isotropic. The theorem then follows by an induction on the degree of the
extension, ultimately reducing the problem to degree one, which is the base
ring itself.
This geometric proof is powerful because it avoids the need for 2 to be
invertible and relies intrinsically on the structural properties of reductive
group schemes and their homogeneous spaces. Furthermore, we demonstrate
that this generalized Springer theorem implies several strong cancellation
and restriction properties for quadratic forms and the Witt group of LG
rings, 9.1(b).
We also provide an analogous result for Hermitian spaces
over quadratic ´etale extensions, showing that isotropy over an odd-degree

5
extension descends to the base ring, thereby illustrating the robust nature
of this geometric method, 9.3.
Norm Principles of Scharlau and Knebusch, §10 and §11. Another major
theme explored in these notes concerns the behavior of similarity factors
and values of quadratic forms under finite ´etale extensions, formalized in
the norm principles of Scharlau and Knebusch. These principles quantify
the extent to which data defined over an extension can be ”pushed down”
to the base ring via the norm map.
Scharlau’s Norm Principle deals with the similarity of quadratic forms.
Let (M, q) be a quadratic space over a semilocal ring R, let S be a finite
´etale R–algebra, and let G(q) denote the group of similarity factors of q
(units u ∈R× such that uq ∼= q). The principle asserts that if u ∈S×
is a similarity factor for the extended form qS, then its norm NS/R(u) is a
similarity factor for the base form q, that is, NS/R(G(qS)) ⊂G(q), 10.13.
To establish Scharlau’s principle, we first prove it for one-generated ex-
tensions and forms of odd rank, where G(qS) = S×2. The general case for
semilocal rings is then achieved through a “Noetherian reduction”. We ex-
press the semilocal ring R as a direct limit of semilocalizations of finitely
generated Z-algebras. By passing to an ind-´etale extension where the residue
fields are infinite, we can find a primitive element for the algebra and apply
the one-generated case. The result then descends back to the original ring
via limit arguments, demonstrating a quintessential application of algebraic
geometry techniques to solve purely algebraic problems over rings.
Knebusch’s Norm Principle is intimately related but focuses on the val-
ues represented by the quadratic form. Let D(q) be the set of values q(x)
for vectors x ∈M such that q(x) ∈R×. We define D(q)[ev] (respectively
D(q)[od]) as the union of products of an even (respectively odd) number of
values. Knebusch’s principle states that for a semilocal R and a finite ´etale
extension S/R of constant degree d, the norm of the values represented by
qS is contained in D(q)[ev] if d is even, and in D(q)[od] if d is odd, 11.1.
The proof of Knebusch’s principle is arguably the most technically de-
manding part of these notes. It relies on the construction of “Knebusch
functors”.
which are intricate refinements of the Springer functors used
earlier. These functors are represented by smooth quasi-compact schemes,
11.11, By proving that these Knebusch schemes are non-empty over semilo-
cal rings (again using geometric properties like schematic denseness and
Weil restrictions), 11.12, we can systematically decompose the norm of a
represented value into a product of values represented over the base ring.
Applications, §12–§14. These norm principles are not merely structural
curiosities; they have profound cohomological consequences. For instance,
we apply Knebusch’s principle to evaluate the spinor norm map sn: SΓ(q) →
R×, x 7→xx where SΓ(q) is the special Clifford group, (5.18.2). We prove
that over a semilocal ring R, the image of the spinor norm sn coincides
precisely with the group D(q)[ev], 12.7. This explicit identification is then

1. Preliminaries
1.1. Faithfully projective modules ([GN2, 1.5]). Recall that an R–module
M is faithful if the structure map R →EndR(M), r 7→r IdM, is injective.
It is a standard fact in commutative algebra, see for example [Bas2, IX,
Prop. 4.6, page 476], that the following conditions are equivalent for a finite
projective R–module P:
(i) P is faithful;
(ii) every localization Pp, p ∈Spec(R), is non-zero;
(iii) PR/m ̸= {0} for every maximal ideal m ◁R;
(iv) P is faithfully flat;
(v) there exists an R–module Q such that P ⊗RQ ∼= Rn for some n ∈N+.
In this case, P is called faithfully projective.
1.2. Direct products of base rings. Let R = R0 × · · · × Rn be a direct
product of rings. We put εi = (0, . . . , 0, 1Ri, 0, . . . , 0). Associating with an
R–module M the direct product
(1.2.1)
(ε0M, . . . , εnM) = (M0, . . . , Mn)
and with an R–linear map f : M →N the direct product (ε1f, . . . , εnf) of
Ri–linear maps gives rise to an equivalence between the category ModR of
R–modules and the direct product of the categories ModRi of Ri–modules,
which we take as an identification:
(1.2.2)
ModR = ModR0 × · · · × ModRn.
The standard algebraic constructions respect the decomposition (1.2.2), for
example the symmetric algebras do so:
(1.2.3)
SR(M) = SR0(M0) × · · · × SRn(M0).
Similarly, properties of R–modules M are reflected by the corresponding
properties of the Ri–modules Mi. For example, an R–module M is finite
projective (faithfully projective resp.) if and only if all Mi are finite projec-
tive (faithfully projective resp.) Ri–modules. Or, A is a unital associative
R–algebra if and if the Ai are unital associative R–algebras.
Let S = Spec(R) and Si = Spec(Ri). We know S = S0 ⊔· · · ⊔Sn, B.1(j).
For a finite projective R–module M the associated S–scheme W(M), see
B.1(b), is the disjoint union of Si–schemes,
(1.2.4)
W(M) = W(M0) ⊔· · · ⊔W(Mn),
which is immediate from W(M) = Spec
 SR(M ∨)

and (1.2.3).
A standard way to obtain ta situation as above occurs by letting M =
M0 ×· · ·×Mn be the rank decomposition of a finite projective R–module M
for which Mi, 0 ≤i ≤n, is a finite projective Ri–module of constant rank i.
The discussion above then describes the reduction of quadratic modules to
quadratic modules of constant rank. We will refer to the process of passing
from M to the Mi as reduction to constant rank.

1.3. Unimodular vectors ([GPR2, 9.13-9.17], [Lo3, 0.3]). Let M be an
R–module and let M∗be its dual space. An x ∈M is called unimodular if
the following equivalent conditions are fulfilled:
(i) R · x is a free R–module of rank 1 and a direct summand of M,
(ii) there exists φ ∈M∗for which φ(x) = 1.
If M is finitely generated projective, then (i) and (ii) are equivalent to (iii)
and (iv) below:
(iii) x ⊗1κ(p) ̸= 0 for all p ∈Spec(R), where κ(p) is the residue field of
the local ring Rp,
(iv) x ⊗1R/m ̸= 0 for every maximal m ∈Spec(R).
We denote by Mu the set of unimodular vectors of M, and list some prop-
erties of unimodular vectors.
(a) For I ⊂Jac(R) an ideal, the map
(1.3.1)
Mu ↠(M/IM)u,
m 7→m ⊗1R/I
is surjective. Indeed, this easily follows from surjectivity of M ↠M/IM,
bijectivity of the maximal spectra, given by Specmax(R) →Specmax(R/I),
m 7→m/I, and condition (iv). Of course, this is also a special case of the
general criterion B.3, as explained in B.4.
(b) (Direct products of base rings) Let R = R0 × · · · × Rn be a direct
product of rings, and let M = M0 × · · · × Mn be an R–module as in 1.2.
Then m = (m0, . . . , mn) with mi ∈Mi is unimodular in the R–module M
if and only if mi is unimodular in the Ri–module Mi for i = 0, . . . , n.
(c) (Unimodular vectors in free modules) Suppose M is a free R–module
of finite rank, say with basis (e1, . . . , en). Then a vector x = r1e1+· · ·+rnen
is unimodular if and only if the ideal (r1, . . . rn) generated by the coefficients
ri equals R.
(d) (Example liner forms) Let M be a reflexive, e.g., finite projective
R–module.
A linear form λ: M →R is surjective if and only if λ is a
unimodular element of M∗.
Indeed, let cM : M →M∗∗be the canonical isomorphism.
Then λ is
surjective if and only if there exists m ∈M such that λ(m) = cM(m)(λ) = 1.
By (ii), the latter condition is equivalent to λ being unimodular in M∗.
1.4. Lemma (The scheme W(M)\{0} = W(M)u, [Lo3, 0.12]). Let M be a
finite locally free R–module. The R–functor T 7→(M ⊗R T)u is represented
by the open subscheme W(M) \ {0} of the affine R–scheme W(M), often
abbreviated as W(M)u. It has the following properties.
(i) W(M)u is a quasi-compact, equivalently, a finitely presented open
subscheme of W(M).
(ii) Forming W(·)u commutes with base change, i.e., for T ∈R-alg we
have W(M)u ×R T ∼= W(M ⊗R T)u.
(iii) If M has constant rank 1, then W(M)u is an affine scheme.

1.5. Semilocal rings. Recall that a unital commutative ring R is semilocal
if it has only a finite number of maximal ideals, equivalently, R/Jac(R) is a
finite direct product of fields, see e.g. [B:AC, II, §3.5].
We list several facts used later, but for which we could not find a conve-
nient reference. Throughout, R is a semilocal ring with maximal ideals mi,
i = 1, . . . , n, and residue fields κi = R/mi. Thus R/Jac(R) = κ1 ×· · ·×κn.
(a) A finite direct product of semilocal rings is semilocal if and only if
every factor is semilocal.
(b) For any ideal I ◁R the quotient ring R/I is semilocal. In particular,
if I ⊂Jac(R), then

A/Jac(R)A ∼= A ⊗R (R/Jac(R)) ∼= Q

1.6. Unimodular rings. In §4 we will encounter the following condition
on a (commutative) ring R. We will say that a ring R is unimodular if every
faithfully projective R–module contains a unimodular vector.
(a) (Direct products) If R = R1 × R2 is a direct product of rings, then
R is unimodular if and only if R1 and R2 are unimodular.
Indeed, this
follows easily from the characterizations of faithfully projective modules and
unimodular vectors in 1.1 and 1.3(b) respectively.
(b) The following is an easy induction:
If R is a unimodular ring and M is finite projective R–module
of constant rank, then M is free.
(1.6.1)
Moreover, using the rank decomposition of finite projective modules as
well as (a) and (1.6.1) allows us to conclude:
Two finite projective modules are isomorphic if and only if
they have the same rank function Spec(R) →N.
(1.6.2)
(c) The following are examples of unimodular rings:
(i) LG rings, in view of (1.7.1), in particular R = 0 is unimodular.

1.7. Review of LG rings [EG], [GPR2], [GN2]. Given S ∈R-alg, one
says that a polynomial g ∈S[X1, . . . , Xn] represents a unit over S if there
exists s1, . . . , sn ∈S such that g(s1, . . . , sn) ∈S×. A ring R is an LG ring if
for every n ∈N+ and every f ∈R[X1, . . . , Xn] the polynomial f represents
a unit over R if and only if for every maximal m ◁R the induced polynomial
fm ∈Rm[X1, . . . , Xn] represents unit over Rm, equivalently, fR/m represents
a unit over any residue field R/m of R. Following are some facts that we
will use.
(a) ([EG, Thm. 2.10], [GN2, 1.6], [MW, p. 457]) Suppose that R is an LG
ring. The following is crucial, implying that R is a unimodular ring:
(1.7.1)
A faithfully projective R–module contains a unimodular vector.
(b) (Quotients) Let a be an ideal of an LG ring R. It is immediate from
the definition that then R/a is an LG ring too. Conversely, if a ⊂Jac(R),
the Jacobson radical of R, then R is LG if and only if R/a is LG. This follows
from the characterization of invertibility and a ⊂m for every maximal ideal
of R.
(c) (Direct products) R = R1 × R2 is LG ⇐⇒both R1 and R2 are LG,
[GPR2, 11.21].
(d) (Integral extensions) If R is an LG ring, then so is every integral
extension of R [EG, Cor. 2.3]. In particular, every finite R–algebra is an LG
ring.
(e) (Examples) Every semilocal ring, for example a field, is an LG ring.
But not every LG ring is semilocal. For example, 0–dimensional rings and
the ring of algebraic integers are LG rings, but are not semilocal.
(f) (Non-examples) The ring Z and the polynomial ring k[X] for an inte-
gral domain k are not LG rings ([GPR2, Exc. 11.42]).
1.8. Rings satisfying the primitive criterion. Recall that a polynomial
in R[X1, . . . , Xn] is primitive if its coefficients generate R as ideal. One says
that a ring R satisfies the primitive criterion [EG, MW] if the following
equivalent conditions hold:
(I) for every primitive polynomial P ∈R[X] there exists r ∈R such
that P(r) ∈R×;
(II) for every primitive Q ∈R[X1, . . . , Xn] there exists (r1, . . . , rn) ∈Rn
such that Q(r1, . . . , rn) ∈R×;
(III) R is LG and all residue fields of R are infinite.


## Page 12

P. GILLE AND E. NEHER (JUNE 30, 2026)
An example of a ring satisfying the primitive criterion, is the ring S−1R[X]
where R is arbitrary and S is the multiplicative subset of all primitive poly-
nomials in the polynomial ring R[X], [vdK, 1.13].
1.9. Proposition (Characterization of LG rings [GN2, Prop. 1.4]). Let R
be a LG-ring, let M be a finite locally free R-module, and let U be an open
quasi-compact subscheme of W(M).
(a) U(R) ̸= ∅⇐⇒U(R/m) ̸= ∅for every maximal ideal m ◁R.
(b) If R satisfies the primitive condition as in 1.8 and U is R-dense (=
universally schematically dense), then U(R) ̸= ∅.
Property (a) characterizes LG rings: if for every finite locally free R-
module M and every open quasi-compact U ⊂W(M) we have the equiva-
lence (a), then R is an LG ring.
Regarding 1.9(b): Since W(M) is smooth, we can by B.7(e) use the cri-
terion (B.7.2) saying that R–dense is equivalent to universally schematically
dense.
1.10. Characteristic polynomials, traces and norms. Let M be a fi-
nite projective R–module.
One can define the characteristic polynomial,
trace and determinant of an endomorphism of M in equivalent ways, either
by embedding M into a finite free R–module [Go] or by faithfully flat de-
scent [KO, II, §2.4], [Fo, 5.3.3, 5.3.5]. All of these generalize the case of a
free R–module treated in [B:A1, III, §8.11].
In the following, let A be a unital associative R–algebra, which is fi-
nite projective as R–module and let a ∈A. The characteristic polynomial
PcA/R(a; X), the trace TrA/R(a) and the norm NA/R(a) of a are defined
as the characteristic polynomial, the trace and the determinant of the left
multiplication La : A →A, x 7→ax of A by a ∈A. They are related by
(1.10.1)
PcA/R(a; X) = det(X IdA −La) = NA[X]/R[X](X −a).
If A has constant rank n as R–module, then
NA/R(a) = (−1)n PcA/R(a; 0)
and
PcA/R(a; X) = Xn −TrA/R(a)Xn−1 + · · · + (−1)n NA/R(a).
(1.10.2)
The following facts are easily established, see for example [B:A1, III, §9.3,
9.4] in case A is a free R–module.
(a) (Base change) Characteristic polynomials, traces and norms respect
base change in the obvious sense: TrA⊗S/S(a ⊗1S) = TrA/R(a) ⊗1S and
NA⊗S/S(a ⊗1S) = NA/R(a) ⊗1S for any S ∈R-alg.
(b) (Multiplicativity) The norm respects products: for a1, a2 ∈A we have
NA/R(a1a2) = NA/R(a1) NA/R(a2). An element a ∈A is invertible if and
only if NA/R(a) ∈R× [B:A1, III, §9.4, Prop. 3].
(c) (Transitivity) Let S ∈R-alg and let A be a unital associative S–
algebra. Assume that S is finite projective as R–module and A is finite

1.11. Finite ´etale algebras. An A ∈R-alg is an ´etale R–algebra if A is
a separable finitely presented R–algebra and flat as A–module. There are
several equivalent conditions defining ´etale algebras, see for example [EGA,
IV4, §17, §18], [Fo], [Len], [Ra2], [St, Tag 00U0] and (a)–(e) below. Below
we list some of the facts we use or that may be helpful for understanding
´etale R–algebras.
We call A finite ´etale if A is a finite and ´etale R–algebra, hence necessarily
finite projective by (c). In [St, Tag 00U0], “´etale” means finite ´etale. An


## Page 14


14
P. GILLE AND E. NEHER (JUNE 30, 2026)
´etale R–algebra A of degree d ∈N+ is a finite ´etale R–algebra whose under-
lying R–module is projective of constant rank d. We say that A is faithfully
´etale if A is a faithfully projective and ´etale R–algebra, hence in particular
finite ´etale.
(a) (Base fields) An ´etale k–algebra E over a field k is the same as an
´etale k–algebra in the sense of [B:A2, V, §6], i.e., E is a finite-dimensional
k–algebra satisfying E = K1 × · · · × Kn where Ki/k, i = 1, . . . , n, are finite
separable field extensions, [St, Tag 00U3].
(b) (Finite base fields) Let F be a finite field and let E be a finite ´etale
F–algebra. Then NE/F : E →F is surjective. Indeed, E = L1 × · · · × Ln
is a product of finite fields. Hence NE/F (ℓ1, . . . , ℓn) = Q
i NLi/F (ℓi), ℓi ∈Li
by 1.10(d), and each NLi/F is surjective by [B:A2, V, §12.2, Prop. 4].
(c) (Finite ´etale) A finite R–algebra A is finitely presented as R–algebra
if and only if A is finitely presented as R–module [EGA, IV1, 1.4.7]. In
particular, a finite ´etale R–algebra is finite projective as R–module. A finite
projective A ∈R-alg is (finite) ´etale if and only if A is separable.
(d) (Fiberwise Criterion) Let A ∈R-alg be a finitely presented R–algebra
whose underlying R–module is flat. Then the following are equivalent.
(i) A is an ´etale R–algebra;
(ii) for each p ∈Spec(R) the κ(p)–algebra A ⊗R κ(p) is ´etale;
(iii) for each R–field F, the F–algebra A ⊗R F is ´etale.
(e) (Trace criterion) Let A ∈R-alg be finite projective and faithful. Then
A is ´etale if and only if the bilinear form A × A →R, (x, y) 7→TrA/S(xy) is
regular in the sense of 4.1(a). In this case, A is finite ´etale. This follows for
example from [B:A2, V, §8.3, Prop. 3] and (d).
(f) (Base change) If A is an ´etale R–algebra, the S–algebra A ⊗R S is
´etale for any S ∈R-alg; analogously for finite ´etale ([Fo, 9.2.5], [Ra2, II,
Prop. 2], [St, Tag 00U2]).
(g) (Tensor products) If A and B are both ´etale R–algebras, then A⊗R B
is an ´etale R–algebra ([Fo, 9.2.5], [Ra2, II, Prop. 3]); analogously for finite
´etale algebras.
(h) (Direct products of base rings) Let R = R1×· · ·×Rn be a direct prod-
uct of rings. Given A ∈R-alg, let A = A1 × · · · × An be the corresponding
decomposition of A into the direct product of Ri–algebras. Then A is an
´etale R–algebra if and only if every Ai is an ´etale Ri–algebra; analogously
for finite ´etale algebras.
(i) (Transitivity) If A ∈R-alg is an ´etale R–algebra and B ∈A-alg is
an ´etale A–algebra, then the R–algebra RA/R(B) is ´etale. The analogous
criterion holds for finite ´etale R–algebras, [St, Tag 00U2]).
(j) (Reduction mod Jac(R)) Let A ∈R-alg be faithfully projective and
let I ⊂Jac(R) be an ideal of R. Then A is an ´etale R–algebra if and only if
A/I is an ´etale R/I–algebra. This follows for example from (e) and 1.12(a).

(k) (Split ´etale) Let d ∈N+. The R–algebra Rd is ´etale, called the split
´etale R–algebra of rank d. The name is justified by the following fact:
Suppose A ∈R-alg is projective of constant rank d. Then A is ´etale if
and only if there exist a faithfully flat R–algebra T such that A ⊗R T ∼= T d
as T–algebra, [Fo, 4.6.11] or [Ra2, II, Prop. 4]. One can even “split” A by a
Galois extension T ∈R-alg with Galois group Sd, the symmetric group in
d letters. Then A is obtained by Galois descent from the split case, see e.g.
[Sal, Prop. 2.18 and its Remark] or [CF, 2.5.2.4].
1.12. Around Nakayama’s Lemma. Let Jac(R) be the Jacobson radical
of R (recall Jac(R) is the intersection of all maximal ideals of R), and let
a ⊂Jac(R) be an ideal of R. We denote the reduction mod a by a “bar”.
Thus R = R/a, M = M/Ma = M ⊗R R for any R–module M and f =
f ⊗IdR : M →N for an R-linear map f : M →N of R-modules. We let
can = canM : M →M be the canonical map.
(a) ([Knu, II, (4.2.2), (4.2.3)]) Let f : M →N be an R-linear map between
R–modules with N being finitely generated. Then f is surjective ⇐⇒
¯f
is surjective. Moreover, if M has finite type and N is finitely generated
projective, then f is bijective if and only if ¯f is bijective. In particular, a
finite projective R–module M is free if and only if M is free.
(b) ([Knu, II, (4.4.1)]) Let M be a finite projective R–module and let M1,
M2 be submodules of M such that M = M1 ⊕M2. Then M = M1 ⊕M2.
(c) If A ∈R-alg is a finite R–algebra, then Jac(R)A ⊂Jac(R). Hence,
applying (a) to the R–module A, the R–module map f = La (= left multi-
plication by a ∈A), and a = can(a), we get
(1.12.1)
a ∈A×
⇐⇒
a ∈A×.
(d) ([B:AC, II, §3.2, Cor. of Prop. 6 and §3.3, Thm. 1]) Let f : M →N
be an R–linear map between finite projective R–modules M and N. Then
the following are equivalent:
(i) f is bijective,
(ii) the induced (R/m)–linear map M/mM →N/mN is bijective for all
maximal ideals m of R ,
(iii) f is surjective and rank Mm = rank Nm holds for all maximal ideals
m of R.
1.13. Invertible elements. Let A be a unital associative R–algebra. Re-
call that a ∈A is invertible if and only if the left multiplication La : A →A
by a is invertible, if and only if the left and right multiplication by a are
surjective. We put A× = {a ∈A : a is invertible}, and note that invertibil-
ity is preserved under base change, thanks to La ⊗1S = La⊗1S for every
S ∈R-alg. We list some criteria for invertibility, complementing the ones
of 1.10(b) and (1.10.3).
An element x ∈A is invertible if and only if

We call A ∈R-alg one-generated, or one-generated over R in case R is
important to know, if A = R[x] for some x ∈A, referred to as a primitive
element. We will say that A is unit-generated if A = R[x] for some x ∈A×.
The equivalences in (2.7.3) show that a one-generated algebra need not be
unit-generated.
One-generated algebras are called simple or monogeneous in [Fer, Lo3],
and monogenic in [BFP].
For an R–algebra A we denote by PrimR(A) the set of primitive elements
of the R–algebra A. Thus,

In the following Lemma 2.3 we characterize primitive elements of a finite
A ∈R-alg, although later we will only be interested in A finite locally free.
2.3. Lemma (Characterization of primitive elements for A finite). Let A ∈
R-alg be a finite R–algebra. Then the following are equivalent for x ∈A:
(i) x ∈PrimR(A);
(ii) x ⊗1R/m ∈PrimR/m(AR/m) for every maximal ideal m ◁R;
(iii) x ⊗1E ∈PrimE(AE) for some faithfully flat E ∈R-alg;
(iv) x ∈PrimR/I(A/IA) in the setting of 1.12, i.e., x = x ⊗1A/IA ∈
A/IA = A ⊗R (R/I) for some ideal I ⊂Jac(R).
Moreover, if A ∈R-alg is finite projective of constant rank d ∈N+ as
R–module, then (i)–(iv) are equivalent to (v)–(vii):

2.8. Lemma. Let A be a finite ´etale R–algebra. Then a ∈A is a primitive
element of A if and only if disA/R(a) ∈R×.

This is the case if and only if it holds after a faithfully

invertible after some faithfully flat extension. Since A becomes split after
some faithfully flat extension, see 1.11(k), it is no harm to assume that A is
already split. But in this case the claim has been proven in (2.7.2).
□

In the finite ´etale case the representing scheme is universally schematically
dense, using the terminology of B.7(b).

2.10. Remarks. That the R–functor Prim(A) is represented by a finitely
presented open subscheme of W(A) is a special case of [Lo3, Prop. 1.14],
proven there for A a unital Jordan algebra which is finite locally free as
R–module. We have included a proof for the convenience of the reader.
On the other hand, that Prim(A) is represented by an affine R–scheme,
is proven for Noetherian rings R and A in [ABHS, Thm. 1.4]. Moreover,
also Theorem 2.9(c) is shown in [ABHS, Thm. 3.5]. That paper’s approach
is based on the fact that a ∈A is primitive element if and only if the
morphism A1 →W(A) corresponding the evaluation map eva of (2.1.1) is a
closed immersion. This allows the authors to consider primitivity of algebras
over a non-affine base.

2.12. Local one-generation. We say that A ∈R-alg is Zariski–locally
one-generated if there exists a standard Zariski–cover R′ ∈R-alg such that
A ⊗R R′ is one-generated as R–algebra.
The notion of a Zariski–locally
unit-generated R–algebra is the obvious one.
These types of algebras have been considered in [ABHS-II] for R replaced
by a locally noetherian scheme and A by a finite locally free morphism
S′ →S of constant degree. A weaker concept of a fpqc-one-generated R–
algebra is used in [Fer]: A ⊗R R′ is required to only be one-generated as
R′–algebra.
The following Corollary 2.13 of Proposition 2.9 is in the spirit of Corol-
lary 2.5. The equivalence (ii)–(v) is proven in [ABHS-II, Thm. 2.1] in the
setting of that paper.
2.13. Corollary. Let A be a finite locally free algebra over an arbitrary base
ring R. Then the condition
(i) A is one-generated
implies the equivalent conditions (ii)–(v) below:
(ii) A is Zariski-locally one-generated,
(iii) ARp is one-generated for every p ∈Spec(R),
(iv) ARm is one-generated for every maximal ideal m ◁R,
(v) AR/m is one-generated for every maximal ideal m ◁R.
Moreover:
(a) If |R/m| = ∞for all maximal ideals m ◁R and PrimR(A) ̸= ∅,
then the conditions (ii)–(v) are satisfied. Thus, A is Zariski-locally
one-generated.
(b) If R is an LG ring, then the conditions (i)–(v) are equivalent.
(c) If R is an LG ring with |R/m| = ∞for all maximal m ◁R, i.e., R
satisfies the primitive condition 1.8, and if Prim(A) ̸= ∅, then the
conditions (i)–(v) are satisfied. Thus, A is one-generated.
Proof. Since Prim(A) is an open finitely presented (= quasi-compact) sub-
scheme of W(A), the equivalences (ii)
⇐⇒
(iii)
⇐⇒
(iv) follow from
Corollary B.12. To prove (iv)
⇐⇒
(v) we can assume that R is a local
ring with maximal ideal m, in which case the equivalence is a special case of
Corollary 2.5.

(a) Since PrimR/m(AR/m) is a non-empty open subvariety of an affine

If R is an LG ring, then (i)–(v) are equivalent by Corollary B.12, which

□
Remarks. We note that Prim(A) ̸= ∅whenever PrimR(A) is univer-

We can now extend (2.7.3) to the non-split case.
2.14. Corollary. Let R be an LG ring and let E ∈R-alg be a finite ´etale
R–algebra such that rank E < |k| for every residue field k of R. Then E is
unit-generated.
Proof. By Corollary 2.13(b) and the remark above, it suffices to consider
the case that R = k is a field. By 2.1(f), we can assume that k is a finite
field, say k = Fq. Also, we know from 1.11(a) that E = K1 ×· · ·×Kr, where
every Ki is a finite separable extension of k. By Lemma 2.2 it is enough to
consider the case that the Ki are pairwise isomorphic, say Ki ∼= Fqn, thus
rank E = rn. In this case, the result follows from 2.1(c) and the fact that
the number of irreducible monic polynomials in Fq[X] is bounded by q/n,
or by specializing the formula [FRS, Thm. 1.2].
□
In the remainder of this section we will consider special types of primitive
elements.

2.17. Proposition. Let R be an LG ring, let R′ ∈R-alg be finite ´etale over
R, let A′ ∈R′-alg be finite ´etale over R′ of constant rank, and let u′ ∈R′×.
Suppose that
(i) for every maximal ideal m ◁R with k = R/m finite, the k–algebra
R′ ⊗R k is unit-generated.
Then there exists a′ ∈A′ such that NA′/R′(a′)u′ ∈PrimR(R′)×.
Proof. Let A = RR′/R(A′) be the Weil restriction of A′ (= the R–algebra
obtained from A′ by restriction scalars to R).
By 1.11(i), A is a finite
´etale R–algebra. In part (I) of the proof we will construct a quasi-compact
open subscheme U of the R–scheme W(A) such that the proposition’s claim
means U(R) ̸= ∅; in part (II) we will then show that U(R/m) ̸= ∅for every
maximal ideal m of R, so that we can conclude with the characterization
1.9(a) of LG rings.
(I) In this part of the proof it suffices to assume that R′ ∈R-alg and

3. Algebras over semilocal rings
The following result is folklore. We include a proof since we could not
find a reference.
3.1. Lemma. Let F be a field which is finitely generated over its prime field.
Then for every n ∈N+ there exists a cyclic field extension of F of degree n.
Proof. Let P be the prime field of F and let F ♭be the algebraic closure of P
in F. We will first show that there exists a cyclic extension E♭/F ♭of degree
n.
By [B:A2, V, §14.7, cor. 1 of prop. 17], the field extension F ♭/P is finite,
hence F ♭is a finite field or a number field.
In the first case we take as
E♭/F ♭the unique field extension of degree n, well-known to exist and to
be a cyclic extension. Let now F ♭be a number field. Let p1 and p2 be
distinct prime numbers and let E1 and E2 be cyclic extensions of degree
pr1
1
and pr2
2
respectively. The compositum of E1 and E2 of F ♭is a cyclic
extension of degree pr1
1 pr2
2 by [B:A2, V, §10.8, Thm. 5]. Hence, without loss
of generality, it suffices to consider n = pr for some prime p. Let F ♭(µp∞) be
the extension of F ♭, obtained by adjoining all roots of unity of pth–power
order. It is known ([NSW, XI.1]) that F ♭(µp∞)/F ♭is a Galois extension
whose Galois group is isomorphic to Zp × Γ where Γ is a finite abelian
group. The fixed points under the subgroup prZp × Γ is therefore a cyclic
extension E♭/F ♭of degree pr.
Finally, we extend the cyclic extension E♭/F ♭to F: since F ♭is alge-
braically closed in F, the tensor product E♭⊗F ♭F is a field [B:A2, V, §17.5,
Prop. 9]. It is a cyclic extension of F of degree n = pr by [B:A2, V, §10.8,
Thm. 5].
□
3.2. Lemma ([Fo, 10.1.1] for local rings). Let R be a semilocal ring and let
S ∈R-alg be a finite R–algebra. Then S is a semilocal ring and an integral
extension of R.
Moreover, let m ◁R be a maximal ideal of R and let n1, . . . , na be the
maximal ideals of S lying over m. Put eS = S/mS and denote by eni ◁eS the
image of ni in eS. Then the canonical map
(3.2.1)
eS
∼
−→eSen1 × · · · × eSena
is an isomorphism of S–algebras. Each eSeni, 1 ≤i ≤a, is a local Artinian
ring. In particular, if eS ∼= S ⊗R (R/m) is a field, then a = 1.
Proof. By 1.5(c), S is a semilocal ring. Also, S/R is an integral extension,
see e.g. [B:AC2, V, §1.1, Prop. 1]. It follows that for any prime ideal p ◁R
there exists a prime ideal q ◁S lying over p, i.e., p = q ∩R, and for such a
pair p is maximal if and only if q is maximal ([B:AC2, V, §2.1, Thm. 1 and
Prop. 1]). Since S is semilocal, there are only finite many ideals lying over
m.

Because eS is a finitely generated (R/m)–vector space, it is an Artinian
ring. Its maximal ideal are precisely the ideals en1, . . . , ena. The isomorphism
(3.2.1) is therefore part of the structure theorems for Artinian rings, see e.g.
[Fo, Thm. 3.2.11].
□
3.3. Semilocalization. We remind the reader of the semilocalization pro-
cess. Let A be a commutative (unital) ring and let p1, . . . pc be prime ideals
of A. The subset S = T
i=1,...,c(A \ pi) = A \ S
i=1,...,c pi is multiplicative
and the localization S−1A is a semilocal ring [B:AC, II, §3.5, Prop. 17]. If
q1, . . . , qb are the distinct maximal elements of the p1, . . . , pc with respect to
inclusion, the maximal ideals of S−1A are the S−1qi, 1 ≤i ≤b, and these
maximal ideals are pairwise distinct. We call a ring B a semilocalization
of A, if there are prime ideal p1, . . . , ps of A such that B ∼= T −1A with
T = T
i=j,...,s(A \ pj).
If R is a semilocal ring with maximal ideals m1, . . . , mc, then R× = R \ S
with S = S
i=1,...,c mi by 1.12(a). Hence the canonical map R
∼
−→S−1R is
an isomorphism. In other words, R coincides with the semilocalization at
its maximal ideals.
3.4. Proposition. Let R be a semilocal ring and let ℓbe a prime number.
We denote by κ1, . . . , κc the residue fields of the maximal ideals m1, . . . , mc
of R.
(a) Then there exists a sequence
R = R0 ⊂R1 ⊂R2 ⊂· · ·
of semilocal rings such that for every j ≥0 the following holds.
(i) Rj+1 is finite ´etale of degree ℓover Rj, equivalently, Rj is finite ´etale
of degree ℓj over R;
(ii) Rj+1 = Rj[uj] for some uj ∈R×
j ;
(iii) if κi is finite, Rj ⊗R κi is a field of degree ℓj over κi.
(b) Furthermore, if R is the semilocalization of a finitely generated (com-
mutative unital) Z–algebra, we can additionally require the following three
conditions:
(iv) For every n ∈N and every i, 1 ≤i ≤c, there exists exactly one
maximal ideal of Rn lying over mi, i.e., the rings Rn, n ∈N, have
exactly c maximal ideals;
(v) the algebras Rj ⊗R κi’s are fields of dimension ℓj over κi for all i, j.
(vi) R∞= lim

residue fields are infinite.
Proof. (a) We construct the ´etale extensions Rj+1 satisfying (i)–(iii) induc-

of degree ℓj. Being finitely generated as R–modules, these extensions are
semilocal rings by Lemma 3.2. Let ¯R = R/Jac(R) = κ1 × · · · × κc. Since
R[X] →¯R[X] = κ1[X] × · · · × κc[X] is surjective, it is in view of Lemma 2.3

3.7. Corollary. Let k be a field, let A be a multiplicity-free ´etale k–algebra,
and let a ∈A×. Then there exists b ∈A× such that ab2 ∈Primk(A).
Proof. We know A = K1×· · ·×Kn, where Ki/k are separable field extensions
of finite degree. Since A is multiplicity-free, Lemma 2.2 says Primk(A) =
Primk(K1) × · · · × Primk(Kn). Hence, it suffices to prove the claim for each
factor Ki/k. For them the claim follows from Lemma 3.6 if k is finite, and
from Lemma 2.16 if k is infinite.
□
Our next aim is to prove a version of Corollary 3.7 in case A is not
multiplicity-free, see Proposition 3.9.

3.10. Proposition ([BFP]). Let R be a semilocal ring, let S be a faithful
´etale R–algebra and let N ∈N+. Then there exists an R–algebra T such
that
(i) T is finite ´etale, unit-generated and has constant odd rank,
(ii) the R–algebra S ⊗R T is unit-generated and
(iii) all residue fields of S ⊗R T have cardinality ≥N.
We recall, 1.11(g), that S ⊗R T is a finite ´etale R–algebra. For “one-
generated” instead of “unit-generated” and for 2 ∈R× the proposition is
[BFP, Prop. 7.3]. The proof in the unit-generated case is a straightforward
modification of the proof in [BFP], which in fact does not make use of
2 ∈R×. It is included here for the convenience of the reader.

4. Regular and nonsingular quadratic forms
We establish our terminology for quadratic and bilinear forms, review
some known results and add several new ones. We will use the notation
established in §1.
4.1. Symmetric bilinear forms. A symmetric bilinear R–module is a pair
(M, b) consisting of a finitely generated projective R–module and a symmet-
ric R–bilinear form b: M × M →R. Since we will only consider bilinear
forms that are symmetric, we often simply speak of bilinear R–modules or
just bilinear modules if R is clear from the context. When M is clear from
the context or unimportant, we will sometimes write b for (M, b).
Given two bilinear modules (M1, b1) and (M2, b2) we call an R–linear
map f : M1 →M2 an isometry if f is bijective and b2

(c) (Characterizations of regularity) The following are equivalent for a
bilinear module (M, b):
(i) b is regular;
(ii) bS is regular for every S ∈R-alg;
(iii) bK is regular for all algebraically closed fields K ∈R-alg;
(iv) bRm is regular for all maximal m ∈Spec(R);
(v) bR/m is regular for all maximal m ∈Spec(R);
(vi) there exists a Zariski cover (f1, . . . , fn) of R such that every bRfi,
i = 1, . . . , n, is regular;

(f) (Metabolic spaces) Let (U, b) be a bilinear module. The metabolic space
associated with (U, b) is the bilinear module M(U, b) = (U ⊕U∗, bM(U,b))
whose bilinear form bM(U,b) is defined by
bM(U,b)(u + φ, v + ψ) = b(u, v) + φ(v) + ψ(u).
It is a regular bilinear form. We say a bilinear module (M, b) is metabolic
if there exists a bilinear module (U, bU) such that (M, b) ∼= M(U, bU). A
regular bilinear module (M, b) is metabolic if and only if M contains a totally
isotropic complemented submodule V with V = V ⊥, a so-called Lagrangian,
[Bae, I, Thm. (4.6)], see 4.15 for Lagrangians of quadratic modules.
(g) (Unimodularity) Let (M, b) be a bilinear module and assume x, y ∈M
satisfy b(x, y) = 1. Then x and y are unimodular by (ii) of 1.3,
M = Rx ⊕(Ry)⊥= Ry ⊕(Rx)⊥,
and (Rx)⊥is finite projective with rankp(Rx)⊥= rankp(M) −1 for p ∈
Spec(R).

4.4. Quadratic forms. (a) A quadratic form (over R) is a pair (M, q)
consisting of a finite projective R–module M and a map q: M →R satisfying
q(rm) = r2q(m) for all r ∈R and m ∈M and for which the polar form
bq, defined by bq(m, m′) = q(m + m′) −q(m) −q(m′) for m, m′ ∈M, is a
(symmetric) bilinear form on M.
We often abbreviate (M, q) = q and refer to (M, q) as a quadratic module.
We call (M, q) a faithful quadratic R–module if M is a faithfully projective

R–module, 1.1. We say that (M, q) is a quadratic module of constant rank
or of rank n ∈N, if M has that property.
If (M, q) is a quadratic module and U ⊂M is a submodule, then
U⊥= {m ∈M : bq(m, u) = 0 for all u ∈U}
as in Lemma 4.2.
An isometry f : (M1, q1) →(M2, q2) is an R–linear isomorphism f : M1 →
M2 satisfying q2 ◦f = q1. If such an f exists, we use the symbol (M1, q1) ∼=
(M2, q2) to indicate this. If (M1, q1) = (M2, q2) = (M, q), the isometries of
(M, q) form a group O(M, q) = O(q), the orthogonal group of q.
(b) (Regularity) By definition, a quadratic form q is regular, if its polar
form bq is regular in the sense of 4.1(a). Lemma 4.2 holds mutatis mutan-
dis for quadratic modules using (h) for the concept of orthogonal sums of
quadratic forms.
If (M, q) is a regular quadratic module of constant odd rank, then 2 ∈R×
by [Knu, IV, (3.1)].
(c) (Base change) Let T ∈R-alg and let (M, q) be a quadratic form.
Analogous to 4.1(b) we consider the T–module MT = M ⊗R T. There exists
a quadratic form (MT , qT ) over T uniquely determined by the condition
qT (m ⊗t) = q(m)t2 for all m ∈M and t ∈T ([Rob, Prop. II.1], [Sah,
Thm. 1], or see [GPR2, 11.5] for a recent reference). We often abbreviate
(M, q)T = (MT ), qT ). The polar of qT is the base change of the polar bq of
q, i.e., bqT = (bq)T . In particular, if q is regular, then so is qT .
If f : (M1, q1) →(M1, q2) is an isometry of quadratic modules over R, the
T–linear map fT = f ⊗1T : (M1, q1)T →(M2, q2)T is an isometry of the
quadratic modules over T.
Let qmR be the category whose objects are quadratic modules over R
and whose morphisms are isometries, and define qmT accordingly.
The
assignments (M, q) 7→(M, q)T and f 7→fT define a base change functor
qmR →qmT .
(d) (Radical) The radical of a quadratic module (M, q) is the submodule
rad(q) = {m ∈M : q(m) = 0 = bq(m, M)} ⊂rad(bq)
of M. Given T ∈R-alg, clearly rad(q) ⊗R T ⊂rad(qT ). This inclusion is in
general not an equality, cf. (e).
(e) (Nonsingularity) A quadratic form (M, q) is called nonsingular if
rad(qF ) = 0 for all fields F ∈R-alg. In this case, we call (M, q) a qua-
dratic space.(2)
(2)As for regularity, 4.1(a), it is appropriate to point out that there is no universally
accepted terminology for what we call a nonsingular quadratic form. Our terminology
follows [Sw]. A nonsingular quadratic form is called “non-degenerate” in [Con1], “non-
degenerate” in [EKM], “semiregular” in [CF, Knu] in case of odd rank, cf. (vi) of (l),
“separable” in [Lo2, Lo4, Pe2], and “ordinary” in [SGA7, XII]. If 2 ∈R×, then nonsingu-
lar = regular by (4.4.2). Hence our terminology coincides with that of Lam’s book [Lam].

(i) (Direct products of base rings) Let R = R0 × · · · × Rn be a direct
product of rings.
Recall 1.2: Every R–module M uniquely decomposes M = M0 ×· · ·×Mn
as a direct product of Ri-modules Mi = RiM. Conversely, every family
(Mi)0≤i≤n gives rise to an R–module M = M0 × · · · × Mn. The R–module
M is finite projective if and only if every Ri–module Mi is finite projective.
Let (Mi, qi), i = 0, . . . , n, be quadratic Ri–modules. Then
(M0, q0) × · · · × (Mn, qn) = (M0 × · · · × Mn, q1 × · · · × qn)
(q0 × · · · × qn)(m0, . . . , mn) = (q0(m0), . . . , qn(mn))
(4.4.9)
is a quadratic R–module, referred to as the direct product of the (Mi, qi).
The decomposition (4.4.9) respects orthogonal groups:
(4.4.10)
O(q) = O(q0) × · · · × O(qn).
Conversely, a quadratic R–module (M, q) uniquely decomposes as a direct
product (M, q) = (M0, q0)×· · ·×(Mn, qn) of quadratic Ri–modules (Mi, qi).
The quadratic R–module (M, q) is regular (nonsingular respectively) if and
only if every (Mi, qi) is a regular (nonsingular respectively) quadratic Ri–
module.
A standard way to obtain the situation considered here occurs by letting
M = M0×· · ·×Mn be the rank decomposition of a finite projective R–module
M for which Mi, 0 ≤i ≤n, is a finite projective Ri–module of constant rank
i. The discussion above then describes the reduction of quadratic modules
to quadratic modules of constant rank.
We will refer to this process as
reduction to constant rank.

where φ ∈U∗and u ∈U.
The quadratic form hypU is regular, hence
nonsingular by (4.4.1). In general, a hyperbolic space is a quadratic module
(M, q) isometric to some H(U). Any quadratic module isometric to H(R) is
called a hyperbolic plane.

4.5. The tensor product between a bilinear and a quadratic mod-
ule. Given an R–bilinear module (M, b) and an R–quadratic form (N, q),
there exists a unique R–quadratic form b ⊗q: M ⊗R N →R satisfying
(b ⊗q) (m ⊗n) = b(m, m) q(n), and
bb⊗q(m ⊗n, m′ ⊗n′) = b(m, m′) bq(n, n′)
(4.5.1)
for all m, m′ ∈M and n, n′ ∈N, where bq is the polar form of q. It is called
the tensor product of (M, b) and (N, q), [Sah, Thm. 1].
Sketch of proof. Uniqueness is clear from (4.5.1). To show the existence of
b⊗q, one first defines a quadratic form qF on the free R–module F with basis
M×N satisfying (4.5.1) with m⊗n replaced by the basis element (m, n) ∈F.
One then verifies that the kernel of the canonical map F →M ⊗R N lies in
the radical of qF , defined in 4.4(d), which therefore descends to a quadratic
form b ⊗q with the required properties.
A moment’s thought will convince the reader that the tensor product
respects orthogonal sums, i.e.,
(b1 ⊥b2) ⊗(q1 ⊥q2) ∼=
(b1 ⊗q1) ⊥(b1 ⊗q2) ⊥(b2 ⊗q1) ⊥(b2 ⊗q2)
(4.5.2)
holds for bilinear modules (Mi, bi) and quadratic modules (Ni, qi), i = 1, 2.
The analogous formula for the tensor product between orthogonal bilinear
modules is of course also true. Moreover, the tensor product is associative
and commutative: with obvious notation we have
(b1 ⊗b2) ⊗b3 ∼= b1 ⊗(b2 ⊗b3),
(b1 ⊗b2) ⊗q ∼= (b1 ⊗(b2 ⊗q),
b ⊗q ∼= q ⊗b
(4.5.3)
The tensor product also respects isometries: if φ: b
∼
−→b′ and ψ: q →q′ are
isometries, then φ ⊗ψ: b ⊗q
∼
−→b′ ⊗q′ is an isometry.

4.6. Lemma. Let (M, q) be a faithful quadratic space over R. Then (M, q) is
primitive, i.e., SpanR{q(m) : m ∈M} = R. In other words, q(M) generates
R as ideal. In particular, if r1, r2 ∈R then

r1q(m) = r2q(m) for all m ∈M

Proof. Otherwise, there exists a maximal ideal m containing q(M), whence
qRm has values in mRm and therefore qκ(m) : Mκ(m) →κ(m) is the null form.
Since qκ(m) is nonsingular by 4.4(l), this forces Mκ(m) = {0} which in turn
implies MRm = {0}, contradicting our assumption that M be faithful.
□

4.9.
(Isotropic vectors and (an)isotropic modules). Let (M, q) be a
quadratic module over R. We call m ∈M isotropic or an isotropic vector if
m is unimodular and satisfies q(m) = 0.
Following standard terminology, a submodule N of (M, q) is called a to-
tally isotropic submodule if q(N) = 0. Thus, a totally isotropic submodule
contains an isotropic vector if and only if it contains a unimodular vector,


## Page 48


48
P. GILLE AND E. NEHER (JUNE 30, 2026)
which may not be the case. We call a quadratic module (M, q) isotropic,
if (M, q) contains a faithful complemented totally isotropic submodule. A
quadratic module is anisotropic if it is not isotropic.
The definition of an isotropic quadratic module used here is more general
than the one of [GN1], where (M, q) was called isotropic if it contains an
isotropic vector. See (e) below for a comparison of the two definitions, and (f)
and (g) for a “justification” of the change of definition. We emphasize that
the results of [GN1] remain true with the new terminology since they concern
quadratic spaces over semilocal rings where the two definitions coincide.
We will later show that the R–functor, assigning to T ∈R-alg the set of
isotropic vectors in (M, q)T , is represented by a scheme Yq, 9.5(b). Some
properties of Yq are established in 9.6 and 9.7. Below we list some useful
immediate facts concerning isotropy.
(a) (Base change) If m is an isotropic vector of (M, q), then m ⊗1S is an
isotropic vector of (M, q)S for any S ∈R-alg.
(b) (Direct products) Let R = R0×· · ·×Rn be a direct product of rings Ri
and let (M, q) = (M0, q0)×· · ·×(Mn, qn) be a quadratic R–module where the
(Mi, qi) are quadratic Ri–modules, see 4.4(i). Then m = (m0, . . . , mn) ∈M
is an isotropic vector of (M, q) if and only if every mi is an isotropic vector of
(Mi, qi). More generally, (M, q) is an isotropic R–module if and only every
(Mi, qi) is an isotropic Ri–module.
(c) ([EKM, 7.13] for fields) Let (M, q) be a quadratic module and assume
v ∈M satisfies q(v) = 0. We consider the following conditions (ci)–(ciii):
(ci) the linear form bq(v, ·) is surjective,
(cii) there exists w ∈M such that (v, w) is a hyperbolic pair, see 4.4(j).
(ciii) v is unimodular, hence isotropic.
Then
(ci) ⇐⇒(cii) =⇒(ciii).
If q is nonsingular, then all three conditions are equivalent.
Indeed, if (ci) holds, there exists y ∈M such that bq(v, y) = 1. Then
(v, y −q(y)v) is a hyperbolic pair. The implication (cii) =⇒(ci) holds by
definition of a hyperbolic pair, and (cii) =⇒(ciii) follows from 1.3(ii).
Suppose that q is nonsingular and (ciii) holds, but not (ci). Then the ideal
bq(v, M)◁R is proper, say contained in a maximal ideal m◁R. Let k = R/m.
Then bqk(v⊗1k, Mk) = b(v, M)⊗R1k = 0 and also qk(v⊗1k) = q(v)⊗1k = 0.
Hence v ⊗1k ∈rad(qk) = {0} by nonsingularity of q.
But v ⊗1k = 0
contradicts 1.3(iv). Thus (cii) holds.

(e) If (M, q) contains an isotropic vector, say m ∈M, then (M, q) is
an isotropic module, since Rm is a free, hence faithful, and complemented

Conversely, a quadratic module (M, q) over a unimodular ring R is isotro-
pic if and only if (M, q) contains an isotropic vector.
Indeed, a faithful
complemented submodule is faithfully projective and therefore contains a
unimodular vector by definition of a unimodular ring in 1.6.
(f) (Equivalence with Knebusch’s definition of an isotropic module) Sup-
pose Spec(R) is connected. Then (M, q) is isotropic if and only if (M, q)
contains a non-zero complemented totally isotropic submodule. Indeed, a
complemented submodule of M is finite projective. In case Spec(R) is con-
nected, it is therefore faithful if and only if it is non-zero.
The canonical analogue of this characterization of isotropy for symmetric
bilinear forms is Knebusch’s definition of an isotropic symmetric bilinear
module in [Knb2] and [Knb5].
(g) Let again (M, q) be a quadratic space over R and let SO(q) be the
associated reductive group scheme over Spec(R), see 5.13 for a review. In
Proposition 5.16 we will prove:
A quadratic space (M, q) with rank M ≥3 is isotropic
if and only if SO(q) is an isotropic reductive group scheme.

4.13. Example: The isometry (4.12.3) does not extend in general.
Let H be the real division quaternions. Its norm is the real quadratic form
q4 = ⟨1, 1, 1, 1⟩of 4 squares. Now consider R = R[X, Y ], the polynomial
ring in 2 variables over R, and the Azumaya R–algebra H[X, Y ] = H ⊗R R.
Its norm is q4,R, the base change of q4 to R.

Prop. 1]. The endomorphism algebra B = EndH[X,Y ](P) is an Azumaya
R–algebra of rank 4. Let q: B →R be its norm. It is known that q is not
extended from R ([KO1, Proof of Pro. 2.1]). In particular, q and q4,R are
not isometric.
However, by Karoubi’s Theorem [Knu, VII, Thm. (4.1.3)], the quadratic

4.14. Corollary (Characterization of hyperbolic spaces). Let (M, q) be a
quadratic R–space. Then the following are equivalent:
(i) (M, q) is hyperbolic;
(ii) M admits a direct summand L satisfying q(L) = 0 and 2 rankp L =
rankp M for all p ∈Spec(R);

In this case (M, q) ∼= H(L).

H(L) is hyperbolic and M = (L⊕V )⊕(L⊕V )⊥. Since V ∼= L∗as R–modules,
rankm(L⊕V ) = rankm M by (iii), whence L⊕V = M and L⊥= L⊕(L⊥∩V )
with L⊥∩V ∼= {φ ∈L∗: φ(L) = 0} = {0}. The proof of (iv)
=⇒
(i)
follows the same pattern.
□
4.15. Lagrangians. A Lagrangian of a quadratic space (M, q) is a comple-
mented totally isotropic submodule L with L = L⊥. Corollary 4.14 shows
that the following three conditions (i)–(iii) are equivalent for a complemented
submodule L with q(L) = 0:
(i) L is a Lagrangian,
(ii) 2 rankp L = rankp M for every p ∈Spec(R),
(iii) 2 rankm L ≥rankm M for every maximal p ∈Spec(R).
We list some facts:
(a) By Proposition 4.11, any Lagrangian L is part of a hyperbolic pair
(L, L′) of submodules of (M, q) in the sense of 4.12.
(b) Any R–module isomorphism of two Lagrangians L and L′ of (M, q)
extends to an isometry of (M, q). This follows from (a) and the isometry
(4.12.3).
(c) If R is a unimodular ring, then the orthogonal group O(q) acts tran-
sitively on the set of Lagrangians of (M, q).
Indeed, by (ii), two Lagrangians L and L′ of (M, q) have the same rank
function Spec(R) →N. Hence L and L′ are isomorphic as R–modules by
(1.6.2). Then (4.12.3) says that there exists an isometry of (M, q) mapping
L onto L′.
(d) The group scheme O(q) acts on the scheme of Lagrangians L(q),
defined in 8.2, in the obvious way. Using the terminology of C.8, we get:
The action of O(q) on the Lagrangian quadric L(q) is transitive on the big
affine Zariski site of Spec(R).
Indeed, since the schemes O(q) and L(q) are finitely presented, an appli-
cation of the surjectivity criterion B.11 reduces the proof to surjectivity in
the fibres, where it follows for example from (c).
(e) It is in general not true that SO(q) acts transitively on the Lagrangians
of a quadratic space, see 8.17(b) for a transitivity criterion.

Examples and non-examples. (a) The main example of rings with
Witt cancellation are LG rings ([GN2, 5.5]).
(b) Using 4.4(i), it is easily seen that a direct product ring R = R1 ×· · ·×
Rn has Witt cancellation if and only if all R1, . . . , Rn have Witt cancellation.
(c) (Witt cancellation and base change) Let qsR be the full subcategory
of the category qmR of 4.4(c) whose objects are quadratic spaces over R and
whose morphisms are isometries. For T ∈R-alg define qsT accordingly.
Suppose that T is faithfully flat, and that the restriction bRT : qsR →qsT
of the base change functor of 4.4(c) is essentially surjective on objects and
that isometries in qsT descend to qsR. Since by 4.1(c) the quadratic form
qT is regular if and only if q is regular and since bRT preserves orthogonal
sums, the ring R has Witt cancellation if and only if so does T.
An example of such a situation is R = k a field of characteristic ̸= 2 and
T = k[X], the polynomial ring over k. That bRT satisfies the conditions
above, is Harder’s Theorem. Thus, k[X] has Witt cancellation ([Knu, VII,
(2.3.2)]). We point out that k[X] is not an LG ring, 1.7(f), while k is.
(d) (A non-example) The example 4.13 together with the remark in 4.12
shows that R[X, Y ] does not have Witt cancellation.
(e) (Another non-example) Witt cancellation is in general not true if q
and q′ in (4.17.1) are not regular, as the following examples shows, lifted
from [EKM, (8.7)].

4.18. Corollary. Let (M, q) and (M′, q′) be quadratic modules and assume
that (M, q) is regular.
(a) [Bae, I, (4.7.i)] If (M, q) and (M′, q′) are isometric, the quadratic
module (M, q) ⊥(M′, −q′) is hyperbolic: (M, q) ⊥(M′, −q′) ∼= H(M).
(b) (M′, q′) is regular if and only if (M′, q′) ⊥(M′, −q′) ∼= H(M′).
(c) If R has Witt cancellation, for example if R is an LG ring, and (M′, q′)
is nonsingular, then
(M, q) ⊥(M′, −q′) ∼= H(M)
⇐⇒
(M, q) ∼= (M′, q′).

Remark.
Corollary 4.18 is not true for nonsingular quadratic forms,
even over fields. For example, let (M, q) = (F, ⟨u⟩q) = (M′, q′) with F a

but 0 ̸= (1F , 1F ) ∈rad(q ⊥(−q)), so that q ⊥(−q) is singular, hence in
particular not hyperbolic.

for quadratic spaces over fields [EKM, Thm. 8.5] and for regular symmetric
bilinear modules [Knb5, I, §3, Prop. 3].

If R is a field, existence and uniqueness of quadratic submodules (Mh, q|Mh
and (Ma, q|Ma) of (4.19.1) is proven in [EKM, Thm. 8.5] with essentially the
same proof. For a general R, we only have a weak “uniqueness”, namely
when the maximal complemented totally isotropic submodules of (M, q) are
isomorphic, see 4.12 and 4.15.
Examples: (a) A nonsingular 1–dimensional form ⟨a⟩q is anisotropic.
A 2–dimensional quadratic form [a, b] is either ansiotropic or hyperbolic.
Hence, in these two cases the Witt “decomposition” is unique for any R.
(b) The Witt decomposition of the quadratic form of the Example 4.17(e)
is given in (4.17.3).
(c) One has of course a decomposition (4.19.1) of a quadratic space over
an arbitrary R.
We have chosen to stick to the connected case since in
general such a decomposition has rather counter-intuitive properties.
A
case in point is the direct product R = R1 × R2 of fields R1 and R2 with
(M1, q1) hyperbolic over R1 and (M2, q2) anisotropic over R2.

The following Proposition 4.22 is inspired by [Col, Prop. 1.2], which proves
it for semilocal rings R with 2 ∈R×. It puts the Corollaries 2.7, 2.8, 2.9 and
2.12 of [GN1] into a general framework. We will say that a quadratic module
(M, q) contains a quadratic module (M1, q1) if there exists a complemented
submodule N ⊂M such that (M1, q1) ∼= (N, q|N). We recall that if (M1, q1)
is regular, e.g., a hyperbolic space, then (M, q) = (M1, q1) ⊥(M1, q1)⊥by
4.2(a) (and 4.4(b)).
4.22. Proposition. We fix a pair (R, S) consisting of a ring R and S ∈
R-alg, and consider the following conditions on quadratic R–spaces:
(A)hyp Given a quadratic R–space (M, q) and a finite projective R–module
N such that (M, q)S contains H(N)S, then (M, q) contains H(N).
(A)reg Let (M, q) be a quadratic R–space and let (M1, q1) be a regular qua-
dratic module such that (M, q)S contains (M1, q1)S.
Then (M, q)
contains (M1, q1).
(B)hyp If (M, q) is a quadratic R–space and N is a finite projective R–
module such that (M, q)S ∼= H(N)S, then (M, q) ∼= H(N).
(B)reg If (M, q) is an R–space and (M′, q′) a regular quadratic R–module
such that (M, q)S ∼= (M′, q′)S, then (M, q) ∼= (M′, q′).
(B)rk1 If (M, q) and (M′, q′) are quadratic R–spaces of rank 1 such that
(M, q)S ∼= (M′, q′)S, then (M, q) ∼= (M′, q′).

4.23. Instances when some of the conditions of Proposition 4.22
are satisfied. Let R be arbitrary and let S ∈R-alg; further conditions on
R and/or on S will be specified below. The conditions (A)hyp,...,(E) of 4.22
make sense for any pair (R, S). We give below a likely incomplete account
of what is known about their validity; see [Col, 2.3] for a discussion covering
results up to 1979.
(a) By 4.9(c), condition (D) of 4.22 is equivalent to the condition
(D′) If (M, q) is a quadratic R–space such that (M, q)S contains a hyper-
bolic plane, then already (M, q) contains a hyperbolic plane.
This is the formulation of condition (D) used in several papers, e.g. in [PS].
(b) (Condition (B)rk1) Suppose Pic(R) = 0, e.g., assume R is unimodular
or even LG. Then condition (B)rk1 of 4.22 is equivalent to the following
condition: Given u, u′ ∈R× and v ∈S× such that u′ ⊗1S = (u ⊗1S)v2,
then there exists y ∈R× satisfying u′ = uy2.
For example, if S is finite projective of constant odd rank, then (B)rk1
holds. Indeed, applying the norm NS/R of the extension S/R to the equation
u′ ⊗1S = (u ⊗1S)v2, we get u′d = ud NS/R(v)2 for d = rank S. Since d is
odd, u′ = uy2 for some y ∈R× follows.
(c) If R is an integrally closed domain, i.e., a normal ring, with Pic(R) =
0 and field of factions S, then (B)rk1 holds. Indeed, as can be seen from
the argument in (b), proving (B)rk1 boils down to showing that a quadratic
equation that has a solution in S already has a solution in R.
(d) (The LG case) If R is an LG ring and S is a finite locally free R–
algebra of constant odd degree for which
there exists a finite locally free T ∈R-alg of constant odd
degree such that S ⊗R T is one-generated as R–algebra,
(4.23.1)
then (A)reg, (B), (C) and (D) hold.
Indeed, since R is unimodular and therefore has Pic(R) = 0, the hypoth-
esis (B)rk1 holds by (b). We will prove the validity of hypothesis (D) in
Corollary 9.2, which follows immediately from Theorem 9.1, commonly re-
ferred to as Springer’s Odd Degree Theorem. Since S is also LG by 1.7(d),
the two conditions (B)rk1 and (D) imply all the others.
We will also show in Corollary 9.2 that the assumption (4.23.1) is satisfied
if R satisfies the primitive criterion and Prim(S) ̸= ∅.
(e) (The semilocal case) If R is a semilocal ring and S is finite ´etale of
constant odd degree, then (D), and hence (A)reg, (B), (C), and (E) hold.
Indeed, (D) follows from 9.2 and (B)rk1 from (b).
(f) (The field case) If R is a field and S/R is a field extension, which is
either purely transcendental or finite of odd degree, then (D) holds [EKM,

4.24. Lemma. Let R be a semilocal domain and let K be its fraction field.
Suppose the following condition (D)hyp on quadratic R-spaces is satisfied:
(D)hyp If (N, q) is a quadratic R–space and if U is a finite projective R–
module such that (N, q)K ∼= H(U)K, then (N, q) ∼= H(U).

(C) hold in full generality if 2 ∈R×.

4.25. Remarks. The proof of Lemma 12.5 goes back to [Col, Prop. 1.2, (F)
=⇒(D)], stated there for a semilocal normal R with 2 ∈R×. The corollary
has been retaken in [Fed, Thm. 3], where R is assumed to be a regular local
ring with 2 ∈R×.
A similar local-global approach as in 12.5 is used in [PS] for R a noetherian
domain of dimension 1 with 2 ∈R× and a quadratic R–space (M, q).
(i) ([PS, Thm. 2.1]) If in addition R is semilocal and if (M, q) ⊗R Rp is
isotropic for all p ∈Spec(R), then (M, q) is isotropic.
(ii) ([PS, Thm. 3.1]) If the singular set Sing(R) is finite and non-empty
and if (M, q) ⊗R Rp is isotropic for all p ∈Sing(R), then (M, q) is
isotropic.

5.2. Theorem (Cartan-Dieudonn´e-Kneser). Let (M, q) be a quadratic space
over a field F. Then O(q) = Refl(q), unless F = F2 is the field of two
elements and (M, q) is hyperbolic of dimension 4.
Remarks. (a) For regular quadratic forms, Theorem 5.2 is proven in
[Di, II, §4, §10] and for nonsingular forms in [Kns1]. An exposition is given
in [Kns2, (3.5)] (see [Kns2, page 19] for the proof in characteristic 2). In
the exceptional case of 5.2 it is easily verified that Refl(q) ⊊O(q), see the

Example 5.10, in fact [O(q) : Refl(q)] = 2 holds in that case ([Kns2, page
14]). We describe Refl+(q) over fields in 5.9.
(b) One may wonder if O(q) = Refl(q) holds in greater generality. Let
R be a local ring with maximal ideal m, and let (M, q) be a regular qua-
dratic R–module. Then indeed O(q) = Refl(q) holds, unless R/m = F2 and
rank M ≤4, [Kns2, 4.6]. But this is not true for an arbitrary nonsingular
q. Indeed, if R = F2[x]/(x2) and q = q0,2m+1 is the split quadratic form of
odd rank, then O(q) ̸= Refl(q), as shown in [CF, 2.6.1.22].
To define the special orthogonal group SO(q), we use the notion of the
discriminant algebra of a nonsingular quadratic form q, which is a subalgebra
of the Clifford algebra of q. We review these concepts in 5.3 and 5.4.

Cℓ(q) = Cℓ0(q) ⊕Cℓ1(q);
its even part Cℓ0(q) is referred to as the even Clifford algebra. We can and
will identify M with a submodule of Cℓ1(q).

Let R = R0 × · · · × Rn be a direct product of rings. As we have seen in

5.4. Discriminant algebras. Let (M, q) be a faithful quadratic R–space(3),
and let Cℓ(M, q) = Cℓ(q) be its Clifford algebra, 5.3. The discriminant al-
gebra Dis(q) of (M, q) is the subalgebra of Cℓ(q) centralizing Cℓ0(q):
D := Dis(q) = Cℓ(q)Cℓ0(q).
(a) (Basic properties) A discriminant algebra D is a quadratic R–algebra
in the sense of [Knu, I, (1.3.6) and III, §4], i.e., its underlying R–module
is projective of rank 2. In particular, it is commutative and carries a so-
called standard involution σD. Discriminant algebras respect base change
and direct products of base rings.
In more detail, let R = R1 × · · · × Rn be a direct product of rings, and let
(5.4.1)
(M, q) = (M1, q1) × · · · × (Mn, qn)
be the corresponding decomposition. The (Mi, qi) are faithful quadratic Ri–
spaces, which are all regular if (M, q) is so. The Clifford and even Clifford
algebra decompose correspondingly, and this gives rise to the decomposition
(5.4.2)
Dis(q1 × · · · × qn) = Dis(q1) × · · · × Dis(qn).
We can use (5.4.2) and the rank decomposition of M to infer properties of
Dis(q) from the known case of M having constant rank.
(b) (The group homomorphism Dis) By the universal property of the
Clifford algebra Cℓ(q), every g ∈O(q) induces an automorphism Cℓ(g) of
the algebra Cℓ(q) stabilizing Cℓ0(q) and Cℓ1(q), and hence an automorphism
of the Z/2Z–graded algebra D = D0 ⊕D1. Thus, we get a homomorphism
of groups,
(5.4.3)
Dis: O(q) −→Aut(D, D1),
g 7→Cℓ(g)|D =: Dis(g).
(c) (Even rank) Assume M has constant even rank. Thus q is regular by
4.4(m). In this case, D is the centre of Cℓ0(q), and a quadratic ´etale R–
algebra [Knu, IV, (2.2.3)]. Hence, by [Knu, III, (4.1.2)], the automorphism
group scheme of D can be identified with the R–group scheme of locally
constant functions with values in Z/2Z = {0, 1},
(5.4.4)
Aut(D)
∼
←−(Z/2Z)R.

algebra D is the centre of Cℓ(q). So D inherits the (Z/2Z)–grading of Cℓ(q),

The next three lemmata are applications of the formulas in Lemma 5.6.
In 4.15 we have seen that O(q) acts transitively on the set of Lagrangians
of the same rank in the LG case. It is well-known, see for example [Con2,
Prop. 3.7], that over fields the action of SO(q) on this set has two orbits.
It is also shown in loc. cit. that SO(q) acts transitively on the set of to-
tally isotropic subspaces that are not Lagrangians. As a first application of
(5.6.2), we will extend this transitivity result to the LG case in Lemma 5.7.

by (5.6.2). Hence ef = fρx ∈SO(q) and, clearly, ef(N) = N1.

5.8. Lemma. Let R be an LG ring and let (M, q) is a faithful quadratic
R–space. Furthermore, let x, y ∈bSq.
(a) The following are equivalent:
(i) y ∈SO(q) · (µ2(R) · x);
(ii) y ∈O(q) · x.
(b) Suppose rankR M ≥2. Then
y ∈SO(q) · x
⇐⇒
y ∈O(q) · x.
under any of the following conditions:


## Page 73


QUADRATIC SPACES OVER SEMILOCAL RINGS
73
(I) q is regular, or
(II) R is a field.
We note that the equivalence in (I) or (II) is not true if rank M = 1, since
then SO(q) = {IdM} while O(q) ̸= {IdM} in general, see 5.5(f).
Proof. For (a) and (b), after applying the rank decomposition 4.4(i) and
using 1.7(c), we can assume that M has constant rank r.
(a) Since (µ2(R) IdM)) · SO(q) ⊂O(q), we only need to prove (ii) =⇒
(i). If r is odd, then O(q) = (µ2(R) IdM) · SO(q) by (5.5.9). If r is even,
then r ≥2 and q is regular by 4.4(m). It is therefore enough to prove ⇐=
in (b)(I).
(b)(I) We write y = g · x for some g ∈O(q) and assume g /∈SO(q).
Since R is connected and g /∈SO(q), we have Dick(g) = 1. Moreover, by
Lemma 4.8, Mx = {m ∈M : bq(m, x) = 0} is projective of rank r −1 ≥1
and qx = q|Mx is nonsingular. Hence, by 4.7, there exists z ∈Mx such that
q(z) ∈R×. The reflection ρz satisfies ρz(x) = x, so that (g ◦ρz)(x) = y. By
(5.6.2), Dick(g ◦ρx) = Dick(g) ⋆Dick(ρz) = 1 ⋆1 = 0 ∈Z/2Z(R), proving
g ◦ρz ∈SO(q) by applying (5.5.7).
(b)(II) If R has characteristic ̸= 2, the quadratic form is regular, and so
the equivalence follows from (I). On the other hand, if R has characteristic
2, then µ2(R) = {1} and the equivalence follows from (a).
□

5.11. Orthogonal group scheme O(q). Let (M, q) be a quadratic module
over R.
The R–group functor O(q), assigning to S ∈R-alg the group
O(q)(S) = O(qS) is represented by an affine finitely presented R–group
scheme denoted O(q). It is the special case S = Spec(R) of the S–group
scheme O(q) associated in [CF, Def. 4.1.0.2] or in [Con1, page 364] with a
quadratic module (M, q) over a scheme S. Below we list some facts that we
will use later.
(a) (Centre) Suppose M is faithfully projective.

is the centre of GL(M)), or see [Con1, Cor. C.3.9]. By (5.1.2), it is an
isomorphism if M has constant rank 1.

5.14. Proposition. Let (M, q) be a regular faithful quadratic module. Then
SO(q) is the identity component of O(q), in particular it is an open sub-
scheme of O(q) and satisfies for all A ∈R-alg that
SO(q)(A) = {g ∈O(q)(A) : gA/m ∈SO(q)(A/m)
for all maximal m ∈Spec(A)}.
(5.14.1)
Proof. We apply the rank decomposition for O(q) and SO(q), see (5.13.3),
and in this way reduce the claim to be proven to the constant rank case
(recall from 4.4(i) that the quadratic forms qi inherit regularity from q). In
the odd rank case, 2 ∈R× by 4.4(b), whence the decomposition (5.13.7)
implies that SO(q) is the identity component of O(q). In the even rank
case, this follows from 5.13(i).
Since the identity component of O(q) is
representable, namely by SO(q), it is an open subscheme of O(q) by C.5.
The equation (5.14.1) therefore is a special case of (C.5.1).
□
Example. Proposition 5.14 is not true, if q is not regular. For example,
let R be the local ring R = Z/4Z with maximal ideal m = 2Z/4Z, and let q
be the nonsingular 1-dimensional form q = ⟨1R⟩. Then −IdR ∈O(q)\SO(q)
because −IdR ̸= IdR and SO(q) = {IdR} by 5.5(f), but (−Id)R/m = IdR/m ∈
SO(qR/m).

We can now characterize isotropic quadratic spaces in terms of isotropy
of the associated special orthogonal group. We refer the reader to C.12 for
some background on isotropic or reducible reductive groups, and to 8.1, 8.2
for the concept of higher rank quadrics.

following are equivalent:
(i) (M, q) is isotropic.

then semisimple. Hence, by C.12(b), the group scheme SO(q) is isotropic if
and only if it is reducible. This proves (iii) ⇐⇒(iv).
□

It is immediate from the constructions above that all three squares commute.
Furthermore, it is shown in [CF, 4.6.0.8, 4.6.0.9] that all rows and columns
are exact in the flat topology.
We present a slightly different proof here. First, it is standard that the
left column is exact in the flat topology. Hence, by commutativity of the
diagram, so is the middle column. It now suffices to show that the first row is
exact in the flat topology, since this will imply exactness of the second row.
Moreover, by (5.18.1), we are left with proving that sπ: Spin(q) →SO(q)
is an epimorphism in the flat topology. For simple notation we show this for

5.23. Similitudes. Given two quadratic R–modules (M, q) and (M′, q′), a
similitude (M, q) →(M′, q′) is pair (f, µ) consisting of a bijective R–linear
map f : M →M′ and µ ∈R× such that
q′ f(m)


We
denote by GO(q, q′) the set of similitudes (M, q) →(M′, q′). If a similitude
(M, q) →(M′, q′) exists, we say that (M, q) and (M′, q′) are similar and call
µ = mult(f) the multiplier of f. For example, an isometry is a similitude
with multiplier 1.
Below we list some easily established facts, where f : (M, q) →(M′, q′) is
a similitude with multiplier µ.

6. Spheres and their smooth loci
We start with a general Lemma 6.1 to be used in the proof of Lemma 6.2,
describing the value schemes of quadratic forms.
We then specialize to
invertible values in Lemma 6.4. We will consider another specialization of
Lemma 6.2 later in section §9 on Springer’s Odd Degree Theorem by taking
zero values.
6.1. Lemma. Let X, Y, Z1, . . . , Zn be indeterminants over an integral doamin
PID A and let 0 ̸= b ∈B = A[Z1, . . . , Zn] ⊂A[Z1, . . . , Zn, X, Y ] = B[X, Y ].
Then B[X, Y ]/(XY + b) is an integral domain. Moreover, if A is a Pr¨ufer
domain, e.g. a Dedekind domain, then B[X, Y ]/(XY +b) is a flat A–module
under the canonical A–action.
Proof. It is straightforward to verify that XY + b is an irreducible element
of the polynomial ring B[X, Y ].
Hence B[X, Y ]/(XY + b) is an integral
domain. Since A embeds into B[X, Y ]/(XY + b), this is a torsion-free and
therefore flat A–module [B:AC2, VII, §2, Exc. 12].
□
For the notion of the content of a polynomial, like a quadratic form, see
B.5; the concept of a universally schematically subscheme is recalled in B.7.

T–points, T ∈R-alg, by
V q,r(T) = {m ∈MT : qT (m) = r ⊗1T },
is represented by an affine, finitely presented R–scheme Vq,r.

q,r of Vq,r satisfies

In the following we will use the obvious terminology that a nonsingu-
lar submodule or a regular submodule of a quadratic R–space (M, q) is a
submodule V of M for which q|V is nonsingular or regular respectively. A
regular plane is a regular submodule of constant rank 2. Our next aim is

Proposition 6.8 showing that one can embed vectors in quadratic spaces over

6.7. Lemma. Let (M, q) be a quadratic space over a field k of dimension
dimk M ≥3 and let m ∈M.
Then there exists a nonsingular subspace
W ⊂M with dimk W = 3 and m ∈W.
Proof. For later use we will consider the following more precise statement
(⋆) below which, as we will see, holds in all cases except case (e):
(⋆) There exist a regular plane P in (M, q) such that m ∈P.
If (⋆) is true, we are done. Indeed, we have the orthogonal decomposition
M = P ⊥P ⊥where P ⊥is nonsingular by (4.4.8) and of positive dimension
by assumption. Therefore, by 4.7, P ⊥contains m′ such that q(m′) ∈k×.
Since km′ is a nonsingular submodule by 4.8(a), the submodule V = P ⊕km′
is a submodule we are looking for.
(a) m = 0: Then (⋆) holds since (M, q) contains a regular plane by [EKM,
7.29 and 7.32].
(b) m ̸= 0, q(m) = 0: Since then m is isotropic, it is part of a hyperbolic
pair (m, n) by 4.9(d), so that P = km ⊕kn satisfies (⋆).
(c) m ∈Sq,a(k), char(k) ̸= 2: Then regular = nonsingular by (4.4.2),
and hence M = km ⊥M′ with M′ a nonsingular submodule of positive
dimension by 4.8(b). As recalled above, there exists m′ ∈M′ with q(m′) ̸= 0.
Then P = km ⊕km′ satisfies (⋆).
(d) m ∈Ssm
a,q(k), char(k) = 2: Then there exists m′ ∈M such that
bq(m, m′) = 1 and P = km ⊕km′ is regular.
(e) m ∈Sa,q(k) \ Ssm
q,a(k), char(k) = 2: Then m ∈rad bq and M = km ⊥
M′ with M′ a regular submodule, [EKM, 7.32]. As M′ ̸= 0, it contains
a regular plane P ′, so that W = km ⊥P ′ fulfills the requirements of the
lemma.
□

We have seen in 4.17 that an isometry between regular submodules of a
quadratic space (M, q) over an LG ring R extends to an isometry of (M, q).
If R is a local ring, isometries extend from more general submodules due to
the following result of Kneser, see [EKM, Th. 8.3] for the field case.
6.9. Theorem ([Kns2, (4,4), (4.5) with H = M]). Let (M, q) be a quadratic
module over a local ring (R, m), and let M1 and M2 be two free submodules
of M satisfying bM1(M) = M∗

2 , where for a submodule

Then any isometry t: M1 →M2 extends to an isometry of (M, q). More-
over,
(i) if R/m ̸= F2 and q(M) ̸= 0, or
(ii) if R/m = F2, and q(M⊥) ̸= 0,
then there exists an extension of t in Refl(q) ⊂O(q).
The assumptions bMi(M) = M∗
i in 6.9 are fulfilled if M1 and hence also
M2 are regular submodules of (M, q), because then bMi(Mi) = M∗
i and any
linear form of Mi extends to a linear form of M since Mi is complemented
by 4.2(a).
For regular submodules, Theorem 6.9 could be derived from
[Knb1, Satz (0.3)]. However, the weaker assumptions bMi(M) = M∗
i are

with nonsingular, not necessarily regular submodules.
6.10. Corollary. Let R be a local ring, let (M, q) be a faithful quadratic
space over R, and let a ∈R×.
(a) Then the group O(q) acts transitively on the sphere Ssm

q,a(k).

q,a(R). By 4.8(a), the submodules M1 = Ru1 and

i = 1, 2, are surjective by 6.5(b), the conditions bMi(M) = M∗
i of Kneser’s

In 6.16(a) we will generalise 6.10(b) to local rings. Its proof uses the case
