reviewed in C.8.
6.11. Corollary. Let R be an arbitrary base ring, let (M, q) be a faithful
quadratic R–space, and let a ∈R×. Then the action of O(q) on Ssm
q,a is
transitive on the big affine Zariski site of Spec(R).
Proof. Since O(q) and Ssm
q,a are finitely presented R–schemes, Lemma B.11
says that it suffices to prove transitivity on local rings. But this follows from
Corollary 6.10(a).
Indeed, we are given an R–ring A and two points y, z ∈Ssm
q,a(A). We want
to show that there exists a Zariski cover A′ of A such that zA′ ∈O(q)(A′).yA′.
In other words, we claim that the orbit map fy : O(q)(A) →Ssm
q,a(A), g 7→
g.y, is an epimorphism of Zariski sheaves. Since O(q) and Ssm
q,a are of finite
presentation, we can apply the surjectivity criterion B.11. It is then enough
to check that fy(Ap) : O(q)(Ap) →Ssm
q,a(Ap) is onto for each prime ideal p of
A. Put B = Ap. Since qB is nonsingular, and since O(q)(B) = O(qB) and
Ssm
q,a(B) = Ssm
qB,a⊗1B(B), this is a consequence of Corollary 6.10(a) applied to
the local ring B. We conclude that there does indeed exist a Zariski cover
A′ of A such that zA′ ∈fy(O(q)(A′)) = O(q)(A′).yA′.
□
6.12. Examples (More examples of transitivity of O(q) on spheres).
Transitivity of O(q) on spheres holds beyond the case of local rings. We
present some easy examples.
(a) (M, q) is a quadratic space over an LG ring R with 2 ∈R×: As the
proof of 6.10(a) shows, transitivity can be viewed as a question whether
isometries between nonsingular (= regular) rank 1 submodules extend to
isometries of (M, q). In the given setting, transitivity of O(q) on spheres
follows from the extension property of isometries in 4.17.
(b) (M, q) is a quadratic space free rank 1 over an arbitrary ring R: Then
(M, q) = ⟨z⟩q for some z ∈R× by (4.4.4). Thus, for u, u′ ∈Sq,a(R) we have
a = zu2 = zu′2, and (u′/u) ∈µ2(R). Therefore (u′/u) Id ∈O(q) sends u to
u′.
One of the goals in the remaining part of this section is to prove transi-
tivity of SO(q) acting on smooth spheres over local rings, see 6.16(a).

7. Transitivity of SO(q) on spheres in the semilocal case
7.1. Theorem. Let (M, q) be a faithful quadratic space over a semilocal ring
R and let a ◁R be an ideal of R contained in the Jacobson radical Jac(R) of
R. Then the canonical map SO(q) →SO(qR/a) is surjective.
For a regular q, this is [Knb1, Satz (0.4)], proven in §3 of that paper. The
proof is elaborated in [Bae, III, Thm. (3.8)] for a = Jac(R). The proof there,
although somewhat involved, could be extended to the case of a nonsingular
q. We leave this to the interested reader. Rather, we prefer to give a more
conceptual proof, using the group schemes SO(q) and Spin(q) which we
have reviewed in §5.
Proof. It is interest to point out that our proof works not only in the semilo-
cal case, but also for an LG ring R for which (7.1.2) holds.
Using the rank decomposition (5.5.3) of SO(q), we can assume that M has
constant rank. Moreover, since SO(q) is trivial for rank M = 1 by (5.5.10),
we can further suppose that rank M ≥2. Then SO(q) is a reductive R–
group scheme.
Next, we will reduce to the case of an isotropic q. To do so, let H = Ru⊕
Rv be the hyperbolic plane over R, 4.4(j), and let (M′, q′) = (M, q) ⊥H.
Then (M′, q′) is a quadratic space by (4.4.8). We put R = R/a and claim:
SO(q)(R) →SO(q)(R/a) is onto
⇐⇒SO(q′)(R) →SO(q′)(R/a) is onto.
(7.1.1)
To prove (7.1.1), let λ be the cocharacter constructed in Lemma 5.17 for
the hyperbolic pair (U, V ) = (Ru, Rv), let P ′ = PSO(q′)(λ) be the associated
parabolic subgroup and let L′ = CentSO(q′)(λ), a Levi subgroup of P ′. Then
L′ = Gm ×R SO(q) by (5.17.2), and the group homomorphism induced by
R →R is the direct product homomorphism
L′(R) = R× × SO(q)(R) →R
× × SO(q)(R) = L′(R).
Since R →R
× is onto by 1.13(iv), the homomorphism SO(q)(R) →SO(q)(R)
is onto if and only if the homomorphism L′(R) →L′(R) is onto, which by
Lemma C.11(a) is in turn equivalent to SO(q′)(R) →SO(q′)(R) is onto.
Having thus established (7.1.1), we will in the following assume that q is
isotropic. In particular, D(q) = q(M) ∩R× = R× and then Dev(q) = R×,
which by (5.20.4) and (5.20.3) implies that the spinor norm SN: SO(q)(R) →
H1(R, µ2) = R×/R×2 is surjective. Moreover, we can also suppose that
rank M ≥4 so that SO(q) and Spin(q) are semisimple group schemes. The

7.3. Examples. (a) The interest in the generality of Theorem 7.1 lies in the
following application. Let again R be a semilocal ring, let S ∈R-alg be
a finite R–algebra and let (M, q) be a faithful quadratic S–space. Then S
is semilocal and Jac(R)S ⊂Jac(S) by [Knu, II, (4.2.4)]. Hence 7.1 with R
replaced by S and a = Jac(R)S says that SO(q) ↠SO(qS/Jac(R)S) is onto.
(b) For special quadratic modules (M, q), surjectivity of SO(q) →SO(qR/a)
holds for more general rings. For example, let (M, q) = H be the hyper-
bolic plane over an arbitrary ring R.
Then surjectivity holds in view of
SO(q) ∼= R× by 5.5.11, and surjectivity of R× →(R/a)× by 1.13(iv).
The analogue of Theorem 7.1 for orthogonal groups instead of special
orthogonal groups is not true, as pointed out by Knebusch in a remark
before [Knb1, Satz (0.5)]: the problem lies in passing from the local to the
semilocal case, where |O(q)/SO(q)| > 2 may happen. However, in the local
case the following holds.

The final topic of this section is the question under which conditions
do we have Refl( q) = SO(q) where, we recall, Refl( q) is the subgroup of
O(q) generated by an even number of reflections, 5.1, and where SO(q) =
SO(q)(R) which always contains Refl( q) by (5.6.2). We have dealt with the
field case in Lemma 5.9 and the Example 5.10. This will now be extended
to semilocal rings in Theorem 7.10 and Corollary 7.12, using the following
results of Knebusch.
7.10. Theorem. Let R be a semilocal ring, let (M, q) be a quadratic R–
module and let γ : (N, q|N) →(M, q) be an isometric embedding with γ(n) −
n ∈Jac(R)M for all n ∈N.
Then there exists g ∈Refl+(q) such that

(i) ([Knb1, Satz (0.1c)]) q|N is regular and q(N⊥) generates R as ideal,

We point out that the condition ⟨q(N⊥)⟩= R in (ii) above is fulfilled in
case q is nonsingular and N⊥is faithfully projective, see 4.7.
For a regular q, part (a) of the following theorem is proven in [Knb1, Satz
(4.3)].
7.11. Theorem. Let (M, q) be a quadratic space over a semilocal ring R.
(a) Then Refl+(q) = SO(q) ⇐⇒Refl+(qR/m) = SO(qR/m) holds for all
maximal ideals m ◁R.
(b) We have Refl+(q) = SO(q) whenever
(i) M has constant rank ̸= 4, or
(ii) |R/m| ≥3 for all maximal ideals m ◁R.
Proof. We will prove this theorem in steps (I)–(IV) below, but not follow
the order in which the claims are stated.
(I) We suppose R = R1 × · · · × Rn is a direct product of rings and let
q = q1 × · · · × qn be the corresponding decomposition of q, cf. 4.4(i). We
know that q is nonsingular (regular) if and only if every qi, i = 1, . . . , n, is
nonsingular (regular respectively). It follows from (5.1.5) and (5.5.3) that
Refl+(q) = SO(q) ⇐⇒Refl+(qi) = SO(qi) for i = 1, . . . , n.
In particular, we can apply the above to the rank decomposition of (M, q)
and in this way reduce the proof of Refl+(q) = SO(q) to the case of M
having constant rank.
(II) Proof of ⇐= in (a): Applying the rank decomposition we can assume
that M has constant rank. We put R = R/Jac(R) and (M, q) ¯R = (M, q).
Then Refl+(q) = SO(q) by (I). Recall Refl+(q) ⊂SO(q) by (5.6.2). For the
proof of the other inclusion, let g ∈SO(q). Then g = g ⊗IdR ∈SO(q) by
functoriality 5.5(a). Therefore g ∈Refl+(q). Since reflections can be lifted

7.12. Corollary. Let (M, q) be a faithful quadratic space over the semilocal
ring R. Then there exists an ´etale extension T ∈R-alg of constant odd
degree with the following properties:
(i) all residue fields of T have cardinality ≥3, and
(ii) Refl+(qT ) = SO(qT ).
Proof. We first use Proposition 3.10 with S = R to get the existence of an
´etale T ∈R-alg of constant odd rank such that (i) holds. We can then apply
Theorem 7.11(b) to get (ii).
□

8. The SO(q) action on higher rank quadrics
In this section we study the R–scheme Qν(q) of higher rank quadrics
whose points are totally isotropic, complemented submodules of a quadratic
space (M, q). We show that such a quadric is always smooth projective, 8.1
and 8.3, and prove in 8.4 that the stabilizer of an R–point is a parabolic
subgroup P of the reductive R–group SO(q). We identify the type of P in
8.6 and 8.7. We also show in 8.4 that SO(q)/P ∼= Qν(q) in case the rank
function ν of the points of Qν(q) is not maximal. The latter case, referred
to as Lagrangians L(q), requires a special treatment which we present in the
second part of this section. Specifically, we identify the Stein factorization
of the structural morphism L(q) →Spec(R). While this is known, even over
an arbitrary base (Deligne [SGA7, XII, Prop. 2.8]), we present a detailed
version of Deligne’s result in the affine setting in Proposition 8.19. This will
allow us to describe the SO(q)–action on L(q) in 8.17 and 8.20.
8.1. Lemma. Let (M, q) be a faithful quadratic module and let ν : Spec(R) →
N be a locally constant function. Recall νR′ = ν ◦(Spec(R′) →Spec(R)) for
R′ ∈R-alg. Following B.15(d), we identify

of complemented, hence finite projective R′–submodules N ⊂MR′ of rank
νR′. We claim that the R–subfunctor Qν(q) of Grassν(M ∨) whose R′–points
are defined as
Qν(q)(R′) = {N ∈Grassν(M ∨)(R′) : qR′(N) = 0}
is a closed subfunctor of Grassν(q), representable by a closed R–subscheme
Qν(q) of the Grassmannian scheme Grassν(M).
Furthermore, Qν(q) is
projective and of finite presentation over R.

once we have shown that Q is a closed subfunctor of G = Grassν(M ∨). To
see this, let R′ ∈R-alg, denote by Spec(R′) the R–functor associated with
the affine scheme Spec(R′) and let f : Spec(R′) →G be a morphism of R–
functors. We need to show that f−1(Q) is a closed subfunctor of Spec(R′).

As a closed subscheme of the projective R–scheme Grassν(M ∨), the
scheme Qν(q) is projective, hence proper and therefore separated and quasi-
compact. It is then of finite presentation as soon as it is locally of finite

8.2. Higher rank quadrics. To avoid degeneracies in rank 1, we assume
that rankR M ≥2 as locally constant function, so rankR M is not necessarily
constant.
We will refer to the scheme Qν(q) of 8.1 as the quadric of rank ν or as a
higher rank quadric if the rank is unimportant. If ν is the constant function
assigning the value d to all p ∈Spec(R) we write Qd(Q) for Qν(q). Two
special cases will have their own name and notation:
(i) If ν is the constant function 1, we abbreviate Q(q) = Q1(q) and call
it the quadric associated with q.
(ii) If rankR M = 2ν we abbreviate L(q) = Qν(q) and L(q) = Qν(q), and
call L(q) the Lagrangian functor and L(q) the Lagrangian quadric,
cf. 4.15.
The group scheme O(q) acts on the higher rank quadrics Qν(q) in the
obvious way. Using the terminology of C.8, we note: The action of O(q)
on the Lagrangian quadric L(q) is transitive on the big affine Zariski site
of Spec(R). Indeed, given R′ ∈R-alg and L, L′ ∈L(q)(R′), there exists a
Zariski cover R′′ of R′ such that LR′′ and L′
R′′ are free, hence are isomorphic
R′′–modules. By 4.15(b), there exists an isometry of (M, q)R′′ mapping LR′′
onto L′
R′′.
8.3. Theorem. Let (M, q) be a quadratic R–module with rankR M ≥2.
Then

Proof. Let q be nonsingular. We aim to prove that then any higher rank
quadric Qν(q) is smooth.
Since smoothness is a local property for the flat topology, we can in view
of 4.4(l) assume that (M, q) is split of constant rank r > 0 and by a possible
further refinement that ν is constant, say ν = n ∈N+. Note 2n ≤r by
Proposition 4.11. Since we already know that Qν(q) is finitely presented
(8.1), smoothness is equivalent to the lifting property ([EGA, IV4, (17.5.1)],
[SGA3, XI, 1.5]): for any local A ∈R-alg and any nilpotent ideal a ◁A the
canonical map Qn(q)(A) →Qn(q)(A/a) is surjective. We can even assume

Let now n be arbitrary. Since P = N ⊕N′ is regular and free of rank 2n,
the decomposition M ⊗R ¯R = P ⊥P ⊥lifts to an orthogonal decomposition
M = eP ⊥eP ⊥with eP regular and free of rank 2n, [Bae, I, Cor. 3.4]. Putting

belongs to the image of Qn(q)(R) →Qn( ¯R).
Summarizing, we have shown that if q is nonsingular, then every higher
rank quadric Qν(q) is smooth, in particular this holds for Q(q). We have
already seen in 8.1 that all higher rank quadrics Qν(q) are projective R–
schemes.
The direction from right to left in (8.3.1) follows from [SGA7, XII], where
smoothness of Q(q) is taken as definition of a so-called ordinary quadratic
form. It is then shown in Prop. 1.2 of loc. cit. that after an ´etale exten-
sion ordinary quadratic forms become exactly the split quadratic forms of
4.4(k), i.e., they are nonsingular in our sense. Alternatively, one can argue
as follows. Let F ∈R-alg be a field. Since smoothness is stable under base
change we know that Q(q)F = Q(qF ) is smooth. But then qF is nonsingu-
lar by [EKM, 22.1]. By definition of nonsingularity, this implies that q is
nonsingular.
□
Our next goal is to investigate the actions of O(q) and SO(q) on higher
rank quadrics.
There are two cases that are already present over fields,
quadrics not of full rank and Lagrangians. In both cases it will be important
that stabilizers are parabolic in SO(q), which we establish in 8.4. In 8.4–8.8
we will assume rankR M ≥3. The reason for this restriction is that if M
has constant rank 2, then SO(q) is a torus by (ii) of 5.13(e), rendering the
theory of parabolic subgroups of SO(q) uninteresting.
Moreover, if (M, q) has rank 2 and contains an isotropic complemented
submodule N of rank 1, i.e., a Lagrangian, then (M, q) ∼= H(N) by Corol-
lary 4.14 and we have already seen in 4.15(c) that O(q) acts transitively on
Lagrangians in the unimodular case, see 8.20 for the SO(q) action.
Proposition 8.4 extends [GN1, Prop. A.5(a)(i),(ii)], dealing with the case
of quadrics. The proof given here is different from the one of [GN1]. A
different proof of 8.4(a) is given in 5.17(b).

8.4. Proposition (Stabilizers). Let (M, q) be a quadratic space satisfying
rankR M ≥3, let ν be a locally constant function with 1 ≤ν(p) for every
p ∈Spec(R), and let N ∈Qν(q)(R). Then the following hold.
(a) The stabilizer P = StabSO(q)(N) of N is a parabolic subgroup of
SO(q).
(b) Assume 2ν < rankR M. Then the orbit map induces an isomorphism
SO(q)/P
∼
−→Qν(q) of R–schemes.
(c) Suppose 2ν = rankR M. Then P = NormO(q)(P) = StabO(q)(N)
and the orbit map induces an isomorphism O(q)/P
∼

schemes.
Proof. (a) We have proven this in 5.17(b), using the dynamic method C.7(f).
We offer a different proof here.
Recall C.7(a) that we need to show that P is a smooth subgroup scheme
for which all geometric fibres are parabolic subgroups of the appropriate
fibres of SO(q). Since these conditions are local for the flat topology, we
can apply 4.4(l) and assume that q is a split quadratic form and that ν is
constant, say ν = n ∈N+. Since split forms arise by base change from Z to
R, we can further suppose that R = Z.
Let Puniv over Qn(q) be the stabilizer of the universal point of the Z–
scheme Qn(q), i.e., in the setting of B.1(c) we have S = Spec(Z), G = SO(q),
X = Qn(q) and H = Puniv. Then Qn(q) is a reduced Z–scheme, cf. the
remark at the end of B.1(h). Moreover, by B.1(a), the Qn(q)–scheme Puniv
is locally of finite presentation since both SO(q) →S and Qn(q) →S are so.
The geometric fibres of Puniv are stabilizers of the corresponding fibres of N
and are therefore parabolic subgroups, hence smooth connected. They have
the same dimension by [Con2, §3], see also 8.6 below. Thus, the conditions
(I) and (II) of B.1(b) are fulfilled, allowing us to conclude that Puniv is
smooth over Qn(q) and then that P is smooth over R. It follows that P is
a parabolic subgroup of SO(q).
(b) According to [SGA3, XXI, 5.8.5], the fppf quotient SO(q)/P is rep-
resentable by a smooth projective R-scheme. The orbit map gives rise to
a monomorphism f : SO(q)/P →Qν(q) between proper R-schemes (recall
that projective schemes are in particular proper [St, Tag 01WC]. It follows
that f is a proper monomorphism, hence a closed immersion [St, Tag 04XV].
For each point s of Spec(R), we know that fs : (SO(q)/P)s →(Qν(q))s is
surjective by [Con2, Cor. 3.4]. Since Qν(q)s is geometrically integral (as
quotient of the geometrically integral κ(s)–scheme SO(q)s), it follows that
fs is an isomorphism. The fibrewise isomorphism criterion B.1(k) enables
us to conclude that f is an isomorphism.
(c) Since M has even rank, SO(q) is the identity component of O(q),
part (i) of 5.13(e). Hence, according to [Gi2, Prop. 3.4.3], the normalizer
NormO(q)(P) =: X is representable by a smooth R–scheme and O(q)/X
is representable. Moreover, from [Gi2, Prop. 4.5.5(3)] we know that X/P

8.9. Lemma (Left ideals in separable algebras). Let B be a separable R–
algebra, and let L ⊂B be a left ideal such that B/L is projective as R–
module.
Then there exists an idempotent c ∈B such that L = Bc and
B = Bc ⊕B(1B −c). Hence, L and B/L ∼= B(1B −c) are projective as
B–modules.
Proof. The exact sequence 0 →L →B →B/L →0 of B–modules is split-
exact as exact sequence of R–modules. Hence, by [Fo, 4.4.1], it is split-exact
as sequence of B–modules. Thus, there exists a left ideal L′ ⊂B such that
L ⊕L′ = B. Decomposing 1B = c + c′ with c ∈L, c′ ∈L′, one finds that c
is an idempotent with L = Bc and L′ = B(1B −c) [Fo, 1.1.20].
□
8.10. Elementary idempotents in composition algebras. Let C be a
composition algebra over R, see for example [GPR2, 19.5]. Examples are
quadratic ´etale algebras and quaternion algebras that we will consider in 8.10
and 8.22. We denote by 1C the identity element of C, and by TrC/R and
NC/R its trace and norm respectively, cf. 1.10. The following are equivalent
for an element e ∈C:
(i) TrC/R(e) = 1 and NC/R(e) = 0,
(ii) e is an idempotent satisfying 0 ̸= (e⊗R1R′) ̸= 1R′ for all R′ ∈R-alg,
(iii) e and e′ = 1C −e are unimodular idempotents,
(iv) e and e′ = 1C −e are idempotents whose Peirce spaces eCe and e′Ce′
are free of rank 1 as R–modules with bases e and e′,
(v) the map φe : R × R →C, (r1, r2) 7→r1e + r2e′ is an injective R–
algebra homomorphism.
Indeed, the equivalences (i)–(iii) are [GPR2, Exc. 16.23], the implication
(iii) =⇒(iv) is [GPR2, Exc. 19.35], the implication (iv) =⇒(v) follows
from ee′ = 0, and the implication (v) =⇒(ii) is clear.
An element e ∈C satisfying the equivalent conditions above is called an
elementary idempotent. If e is an elementary idempotent, they so is e′.
8.11. Lemma (Elementary idempotents in quadratic ´etale algebras). Let D
be a quadratic ´etale R–algebra, see for example [GPR2, 19.19]. We use the
notation of 8.10, and observe that D = De ⊕De′ for any idempotent e with
e′ = 1D −e. Then the map

8.12. The scheme of elementary idempotents of a quadratic ´etale
algebra. Let D be a quadratic ´etale R–algebra. We define an R–functor
Splid(D) whose R′–points, R′ ∈R-alg, are
 Splid(D)

(R′) = {e ∈D ⊗R R′ : e elementary idempotent}
= HomR′-alg(D ⊗R R′, R′) = HomR-alg(D, R′).
(8.12.1)
The second equality above is the identification 8.11, and the third equality is
standard. In particular, the third equality shows that Splid(D) is the functor
of points associated with the R–scheme Spec(D), which is a quadratic ´etale
cover of Spec(R).
Lemma 8.11 says that elementary idempotents of D give rise to a splitting
of D, and, conversely, every splitting of D is given by an elementary idem-
potent. Quadratic ´etale R–algebras are the same as composition algebras
of rank 2 [GPR2, 19.19]. Splitting data for arbitrary composition algebras
C of constant rank are introduced in [GPR2, 25.2]; for rank 2, they are
precisely the elementary idempotents, as in(8.12.1). It is proven in [GPR2,
26.3, 26.5] that splitting data of arbitrary constant rank composition al-
gebras are represented by a smooth affine scheme Splid(C). Indeed, for
C = D quadratic ´etale, Splid(C) = Spec(D) is even quadratic ´etale by the
above identification.
We now come back to Lagrangian submodules of quadratic spaces. We
treat the case of hyperbolic spaces in 8.13–8.17 and, finally, the general case
in 8.19. The following Lemma 8.13, dealing with open subfunctors of the
Grassmannian functor Grassν(M), is well-known in the constant rank case.

Remark.
If M is free of finite rank, the family (UN) indexed by all
complemented projective submodules N ⊂M of constant rank n ∈N covers
Grassn(M), and thus yields a proof that Grassn(M) is represented by a
smooth scheme.
Recall 4.4(j) and 4.15: A Lagrangian of a hyperbolic R–space (M, q) is a
direct summand L of M such that 2 rankR L = rankR M and q(L) = 0. Any
submodule L′ satisfying M = L⊕L′ and q(L′) = 0 is then a Lagrangian too,
we have H(L) ∼= (M, q) ∼= H(L′) as quadratic spaces, L′ ∼= L∗as R–modules
and (L, L′) is a hyperbolic pair of submodules. We call a hyperbolic pair
(N, N′) of submodules of (M, q) a Lagrangian pair if 2 rankR L = rankR M,
in which case (N′, N) is also a Lagrangian pair.

In the following result we use the concept of the Stein factorization of a
proper morphism of schemes, which the reader can find in [EGA, III, (4.3.3)]
in the noetherian case and in [St, Tag 03H2] in general. Any projective
morphism is proper by [EGA, II, (5.5.3)] (or see [St, Tag 01WC]). Thus, the
structure morphism f : L(q) →Spec(R) has a Stein factorization, since f is
projective by 8.1.

8.21. Example: Quaternions. Let Q be a quaternion algebra. Thus Q
is a unital associative R–algebra whose underlying R–module has constant
rank 4 and for which there exists a nonsingular (= regular) quadratic form
nQ permitting composition in the sense that nQ(ab) = nQ(a) nQ(b) holds
for all a, b ∈Q.
It is known that the identity element 1Q satisfies nQ(1Q) = 1R, and is
therefore unimodular; for example, this follows from nQ(a) = nQ(a) nQ(1Q)
and (4.6.1). It is also known that quaternion R–algebras are precisely the
Azumaya R–algebras of constant rank 4, in particular, the quadratic form
nQ is the reduced norm of A in the sense of [KO, IV; §2] or [Sal, 4.3]. We
say that Q is split, if it is so as Azumaya algebra, i.e., if it is isomorphic
to M2(R). The automorphism group scheme Aut(Q) of an arbitrary Q is a
semisimple adjoint R–group scheme of type A1 [CF, (3.5.0.82)]. The Severi-
Brauer scheme SB(Q) is isomorphic to the scheme of Borel subgroups of
Aut(A).

9. Springer’s Odd Degree Theorem for LG rings
In this section we are concerned with the following question: given a
quadratic space (M, q) over a base ring R and some S ∈R-alg such that
the base change (M, q)S is an isotropic quadratic space over S, under which
conditions is (M, q) already isotropic?
In Theorem 9.1 we will give a positive answer to this question for R an
LG ring and for certain S ∈R-alg which force S to be an LG ring too.
In this context it is appropriate to recall 4.9(e): if R is a unimodular ring,
e.g., an LG ring or even a semilocal ring, a quadratic R–module (M, q) is
isotropic if and only if (M, q) contains an isotropic vector. In the proof of
Theorem 9.1 we will therefore consider isotropic vectors, rather than dealing
with arbitrary totally isotropic and complemented submodules. We will also
employ the terminology that “q is S–isotropic” to mean that the quadratic
S–module (M, q)S is isotropic.
We refer to our Theorem 9.1 as “Springer’s Odd Degree Theorem” be-
cause it was first proven by Springer in [Sp1] for R and S being fields of
characteristic ̸= 2. This theorem has turned out to be a fundamental re-
sult in the theory of quadratic forms for which several generalizations exist,
some of them reviewed in the introduction of our paper [GN1]. In that pa-
per we have proven Theorem 9.1 for R and S being semilocal. The proof
given here is different from the one of [GN1], except that we will use [GN1,
Lem. 2.2], dealing with the case of a quadratic space of constant rank 2. For
convenience, we restate that lemma below as Lemma 9.4.
9.1. Theorem (Springer’s Odd Degree Theorem). Let (M, q) be a quadratic
space over an LG ring R, and let S be a one-generated and finite locally free
R–algebra of odd degree.
(a) If q is S-isotropic, then q is R-isotropic. Equivalently, if the reduc-
tive S–group scheme SO(q)S is isotropic, then already the R–group scheme
SO(q) is isotropic.
(b) Moreover, the conditions (A)reg, (B) and (C) of 4.22 hold:
(A)reg Let (M, q) be a quadratic R–space and let (M1, q1) be a regular qua-
dratic module such that (M, q)S contains (M1, q1)S.
Then (M, q)
contains (M1, q1).
(B) If (M, q) and (M′, q′) are quadratic R–spaces such that (M, q)S ∼=
(M′, q′)S, then (M, q) ∼= (M′, q′).
(C) The restriction homomorphisms c
Wq(R) →c
Wq(S) and Wq(R) →
Wq(S) of (4.21.1) and (4.21.3) are injective.
The proof of Theorem 9.1 will be given in 9.15, using the concept of a
Springer functor, 9.12, which we prove in 9.14 to be representable.
The
basic idea of Springer functors can be traced back to [PR, Prop. 1.1]. In
that paper the authors prove 9.1 for certain noetherian local domains R
containing 2 ∈R×.
The approach was refined in [PP] to allow certain


## Page 132


132
P. GILLE AND E. NEHER (JUNE 30, 2026)
semilocal rings R, but still with 2 ∈R×. We have proven 9.1 for arbitrary
semilocal rings R in [GN1, Thm. 2.1]; the proof here is different from the
one in [GN1].
We immediately present two corollaries of Theorem 9.1. The first elimi-
nates the condition that S be one-generated in two circumstances. We recall
that a ring satisfies the primitive criterion if it is LG and all its residue fields
are infinite, 1.8.
9.2. Corollary. Let (M, q) be a quadratic space over an LG ring R, and let
S ∈R-alg is finite locally free of constant odd rank. Furthermore, assume
that
(i) there exists a finite locally free T ∈R-alg of constant odd rank such
that S ⊗R T is a one-generated R–algebra.
Then q is S–isotropic if and only if q is R–isotropic.
Furthermore, assume in addition that Prim(S) ̸= ∅, e.g., assume S is
finite ´etale. Then the condition (i) is fulfilled in the following two circum-
stances:
(a) R satisfies the primitive criterion 1.8.
(b) R is semilocal and S is finite ´etale.
Proof. Assuming (i), the R–algebra S ⊗R T is finite locally free of constant
odd rank and q is (S⊗RT)–isotropic. Hence Theorem 9.1 applies and proves
the first claim.
For (a) and (b) recall 2.9(b) that PrimR(S) ̸= ∅whenever S is finite ´etale.
In the situation (a) we know by 2.13(c) that S is already one-generated, so
that T = R does the job. In the situation (b), the existence of T ∈R-alg
as in (i) is proven in [BFP, Prop. 7.3], see 3.10 for an account.
□
The second corollary of Theorem 9.1 concerns hermitian spaces. As for
quadratic forms, we will say that a hermitian space over an LG ring is
isotropic if it contains an isotropic vector.
9.3. Corollary. Let R be an LG ring, let R′/R be a quadratic ´etale ex-
tension, let (M′, h′) be a hermitian space over R′/R, and let S be a finite

(a) If the hermitian space (M′, h′)R′⊗RS over the quadratic ´etale extension
(R′ ⊗R S)/S is isotropic, then already (M′, h′) is isotropic.

We will prove Corollary 9.3 in 9.18 after a review of hermitian spaces

Springer’s Odd Degree Theorem for quadratic spaces of constant rank
2 holds for more general base rings than LG rings. We have shown this in
[GN1, Lemma 2.2], written in the context of semilocal rings (the semilocality

9.4. Lemma (Rank 2). Let R be a base ring with Pic(R) = 0 and let S be a

of Knebusch functors. We present this next.

9.16. Review of hermitian modules and spaces. We review hermitian
spaces over rings following [Knu, I], see also [GN2, C.1] (but note the change
of notation in order to accommodate the notation of Corollary 9.3).
Throughout R is an arbitrary base ring and R′ ∈R-alg is a quadratic
´etale extension whose standard involution and trace we denote by σ and
tr = Id +σ respectively.
A hermitian module over R′/R is a pair (M′, h′) consisting of a finite
projective R′–module M′ and a hermitian form h′ : M′ × M′ →R′, which
means that h′ is a biadditive map and satisfies

9.17. Lemma. Let R′/R be a quadratic ´etale extension, and let (M′, h′) be
a hermitian module over R′/R.
(a) (Unimodularity, isotropy) Let m′ ∈M′. Then m′ is unimodular in M′
if and only if m′ is unimodular in the R–module RR′/R(M′). In particular,
m′ is an isotropic vector of (M′, h′) if and only if m′ is an isotropic vector
of the quadratic form tr(M′, h′).
(b) (Orthogonal complement) Let U′ ⊂M′ such that h′|U′×U′ is regular.

U′ ⊥= {m′ ∈M′ : h′(m′, U′) = 0}

10. Scharlau’s Norm Principle
The theme of this section is similarity of quadratic forms and Scharlau’s
norm principle, proven in Theorem 10.13 for finite ´etale extensions and
nonsingular quadratic forms.
We start with some essentially known facts on the Scharlau transfer of
quadratic forms.
10.1. Frobenius extensions. We call (S, t) a Frobenius extension if
(FEi) S is an R–ring, whose underlying R–module is finite projective, and
(FEii) t ∈S∗= HomR(S, R) has the property that the symmetric bilinear
form ¯t: S × S →R, (s1, s2) 7→t(s1s2) is regular.
Some elementary properties:
(a) Suppose S ∈R-alg satisfies (FEi) and let t ∈S∗. By the standard
characterization of regular bilinear forms, the following are equivalent.
(i) (S, t) is a Frobenius R–extension,
(ii) (Sm, tm) is a Frobenius Rm–extension for all maximal ideals m ◁R,
(iii) (S/mS, tS/mS) is a Frobenius R/m–extension for all maximal ideals
m ◁R.
(b) ([Bae, I, (2.10)]) Let (S, t) be a Frobenius extension of R. Recall that
for an S–module X we denote by RX the R–module obtained from X by
restricting scalars to R. Then for any S–module M the map
(10.1.1)
t∗: R(HomS(M, S))
∼
−→HomR(RM, R),
f 7→t ◦f,
is an isomorphism of R–modules.
Indeed, let g ∈HomR(RM, R). For fixed m ∈M, the map s 7→g(sm)
is a linear R–form on S. Hence, there exists a unique g′(m) ∈S satisfying
g(sm) = ¯t(g′(m), s) for all s ∈S. It is immediate that g′ ∈HomS(M, S).
We have t∗(g′) = g because g(m) = ¯t(g′(m), 1S) = (t ◦g′)(m). Thus t∗is
surjective. It is also injective: if t ◦f = 0, then for all m ∈M, s ∈S, we get
0 = (t ◦f)(sm) = t
 sf(m)

, hence f(m) = 0 and then f = 0.
Examples. (I) Let S ∈R-alg be finite projective and let t ∈S∗. The
adjoint of the bilinear form τ := ¯t has kernel Ker(bτ) = {s ∈S : t(sS) = 0},
which is an ideal contained in Ker(t). We have Ker(bτ) = Ker(t) as soon as
Ker(t) is an ideal of S. Thus, in general not every t ∈S∗makes (S, t) a
Frobenius extension.
If R = F is a field, then (S, t) is a Frobenius extension if and only if
Ker(bτ) = 0. In particular, if K/F is a finite field extension, then (K, t) is a
Frobenius extension if and only if t ̸= 0.
(II) Let E be a finite ´etale R–algebra and let tr: E →R be its trace map.
Then (E, tr) is a Frobenius extension. Indeed, if R is a field, this holds by
[B:A2, V, §8.2, prop. 1]. The general case then follows from (a).

Remarks.
(1) Injectivity of rS/R : Wq(R) →Wq(S) in 10.4 is part
of condition (C) of 4.22, which is implied by condition (B) of 4.22.
We
have seen in 12.4(a) that (B) and hence (C) hold, whenever R is semilocal
and S ∈R-alg is one-generated and of odd degree. Thus Corollary 10.4
establishes a part of (C) for a not necessarily semilocal R, at the expense of
requiring S to be unit-generated.
(2) That rS/K : Wq(R) →Wq(S) is a split monomorphism and hence
injectivity of rS/R holds in greater generality, namely whenever S ∈R-alg
can be written as a tower R = R0 →R1 →· · · Rn−1 →Rn where each
Ri →Ri+1 is a unit-generated algebra of odd rank. For example, this is so
if R is a field and S/R is a field extension of odd degree [Lam, ].

Scharlau’s Norm Principle holds in case S/R is a finite extension of fields:
It was proven by Scharlau for fields of characteristic ̸= 2 in [Sch1], see [Sch2,
2.8.6] or [Lam, VII, 4,3]. A proof for arbitrary fields, but even-dimensional
forms is given in [EKM, 20.14] (the restriction to even-dimensional forms is
natural in view of 10.10). It was recently proven for fields of characteristic
2 and arbitrary quadratic forms in [Ho, 7.3]. We will give a proof for non-
singular forms and finite field extensions in Lemma 10.11 and for finite ´etale
extensions of semilocal rings in Theorem 10.13.

11.4. Reductions for Knebusch norm principles. Let R be arbitrary,
let S ∈R-alg be faithfully projective and let (M, q) be a quadratic R–
module. We study some reductions for proving the various Knebusch norm
principles, mainly related to small values of the ranks of S and M. As in
11.2 we will use D′ ∈{D, Dsm}.
(a) (Direct products) Let R = R1 × · · · × Rn be a direct product. By
4.4(i), the quadratic module (M, q) uniquely decomposes as a direct product
(M, q) = (M1, q1) × · · · × (Mn, qn), where each (Mi, qi), 1 ≤i ≤n, is a
quadratic Ri–module, which is nonsingular if (M, q) is so. We have D′(q) =
D′(q1) × · · · × D′(qn). An S ∈R-alg which is finitely generated projective
(or of rank d ∈N+ respectively) uniquely decomposes as S = S1 × · · · × Sn
where each Si is a finitely projective Ri–module (of rank d respectively). It
is then easily seen that the smooth/general strong or weak Knebusch norm
principle for (R, q, S) is equivalent with the same principle for (Ri, qi, Si).
For the weak principle, one may use (11.2.6).
In particular, one can find a decomposition of R such that both M and
S have constant rank.
(b) (Reduction to 1 ∈D(q)) If the strong or weak Knebusch norm principle
holds for (q, S), then it also holds for (uq, S), u ∈R×. We can therefore
assume 1 ∈D(q) for proving the Knebusch norm principle.
(c) (Universal forms) If D(q) = R×, the strong and hence also the weak
Knebusch principle holds. The same is true in case Dsm(q) = R×.
The assumption D(q) = R× implies D(q)[d] = R× and then NS/R

⊂
NS/R(S×) ⊂R× = D(q)[d]. The same argument can be used for the smooth

(a) We will show that (M, q) satisfies the assumptions of the general case
(⋆) treated above. Since by 1.7(a) the ring R is unimodular, 4.9(e) shows
that (M, q) contains an isotropic vector. Hence, by 4.9(d), the quadratic
module (M, q) contains a hyperbolic plane H. Since q|H is regular, M has
an orthogonal decomposition M = H ⊕H⊥. By (4.4.7), the quadratic form
q|H⊥is nonsingular, and by 4.7 the quadratic space (H⊥, q|H⊥) contains m
with q(m) ∈R×. Such an m is unimodular by 4.8(a), hence Rm is free of
rank 1 with basis {m} and a direct summand of H⊥. Thus, (M, q) satisfies
the assumptions of the first part of the lemma.
(b) As in the proof of (a) we can split off a hyperbolic plane H and then
know that q|H⊥is nonsingular of rank ≥2. Applying the rank decomposition

spaces over LG rings in 4.20, we see that H⊥contains a submodule N which
is free of rank 2 and such that q|N is regular. Thus, we can decompose
H⊥= N ⊥M′′. Putting M′ = H ⊕N, we get an orthogonal decomposition

The following Lemma 11.12 is crucial for the further development.
11.12. Lemma. Let R be a semilocal ring, let (S, a) be a finite projective
R–algebra of rank d ≥2 and unit-generator a, and let (M, q) be a quadratic
R–space with rankR M ≥3 for which Ssm
q,a−1(S) ̸= ∅.
Then (S, a, q) admits a Knebusch datum, i.e., Kneb(S, a, q) ̸= ∅. Further-
more, if rankR M ≥4, then (S, a, q) admits a special Knebusch datum.
Proof. By assumption we can pick an element u ∈Ssm
q,a−1(S).
(I) We first consider the case that R = k is an infinite field. According
to Proposition 6.13, the smooth S–sphere Ssm := Ssm
qS,a−1 admits an open
non-empty subscheme U := UqS,u, which then is also an open subscheme
of the S–sphere S := SqS,a−1. Furthermore, U is isomorphic to an open
subscheme of P(M ∨
S), hence is smooth and quasi-projective, it is universally
schematically dense, hence also S–dense in Ssm, it has geometrically integral
fibres, and U(S) ̸= ∅.
We can therefore apply B.13(c) and get that Y = RS/k(U) is a non-
empty k–scheme. Also, by B.13(b) and B.13(d), the k–scheme Y is an open
subscheme of the affine k–scheme RS/k(S). It has a k–point since U has an
S–point. It follows that Y (k) is Zariski dense in RS/R(Ssm). On the other
hand, we know from Proposition 11.11 that Kneb = Kneb(S, a, q) is dense
open in RS/k(Ssm) and from Lemma 11.10 that is is non-empty. But then
Y(k) ∩Kneb(k) ̸= ∅.
If instead of rank M ≥3 we assume that rank M ≥4, we know from
Proposition 11.11 that Kneb+ = Kneb+(S, a, q) is dense open in RS/k(Ssm)
and from Lemma 11.10 that it is non-empty.
But then again Y(k) ∩
Kneb+(k) ̸= ∅. We have now proven the claim for infinite fields.
(II) Let us now consider the case that R = k is an arbitrary field. If q is
isotropic, then, by Lemma 11.10, we know that (S, a, q) admits a Knebusch
datum, and even admits a special Knebusch datum, if rank M ≥4. We may

is used to establish injectivity of H1

fppf(S, Spin(qS)) in

, which will
be used in the following section 14 on norm groups.

12.3. Corollary (ˇCesnaviˇcius). Let R be an unramified regular local ring,
let K be its field of fractions and let (M, q) be a faithful quadratic R–space.
Then the maps bO,K and bSO,K of (12.1.1) are injective. In particular, for
a faithful quadratic R–space (M′, q′) we have (M′, q′)K ∼= (M, q)K
⇐⇒
(M′, q′) ∼= (M, q).
Proof. This is proven in [ˇCe, Cor. 9.6] under the additional assumption that
2 ∈R×. The proof in loc. cit. has two steps: (I) A reduction that it suffices
to show injectivity of bSOn,K, where SOn = SO(qn), qn the split quadratic
form of rank n. This is where the author uses 2 ∈R×. (II) Applying the
general result [ˇCe, Thm. 9.1], valid for quasi-split reductive R–groups, to
the split R–group SO(qn). This step does not need 2 ∈R×.
We follow this approach, but replace step (I) by the equivalence (12.1.2)
and then use twisting to replace SO(q) by SO(qn), as already done in [ˇCe].
□
Without going into details, we note that Corollary 12.3 holds for more

12.4. Corollary (S/R odd rank). Let (M, q) be a faithful quadratic space

Then the following hold.

S

Theorem 9.1 and its Corollary 9.2. In case (iv) it suffices to write the finite
field extension S/R as a finite tower of odd one-generated field extensions.
Thus, condition (B)reg of 4.22 holds, see 4.23(e). Moreover, by 4.23(f) also
condition (B)rk1 is fulfilled.
Therefore we also have condition (B). Since
H1(R, O(q)) classifies R–forms of q, the injectivity claim of bO,S in (b) is a
consequence of (a). Then injectivity of bSO,S follows from 12.1(a).
□

13. Reduced norms and traces for Azumaya algebras
The purpose of this section is to generalize some known results on reduced
characteristic polynomials, norms and traces for Azumaya algebras from the
field case, see e.g. [B:A3, §17], to the ring setting. Throughout A is an
Azumaya R–algebra.
13.1. Separable algebras. Let B be a unital associative R–algebra. We
denote by Bop its opposite algebra and observe that B becomes B ⊗R Bop–
module with respect to the B⊗RBop–action given by (b1⊗b2)·b = b1bb2. One
calls B separable if this makes B a projective B ⊗R Bop–module. Standard
references for separable algebras are [Fo] and [KO, III], where the reader
can find more characterizations of separable R–algebras. We list some facts
that we will be using; throughout B is a separable R–algebra. We point out
that the R–module underlying B is not assumed to be finite projective here,
contrary to what is required in [CF].
(a) (Separable versus Azumaya) A unital associative R–algebra A is a
separable R–algebra if and only if A is an Azumaya algebra over its centre
Z(A) and Z(A) is a separable R–algebra ([DI, II, 3.8], or see [Fo, 7.1.11],
[KO, III, 5.5]).
(b) (Base change) The S–algebra B ⊗R S is separable for any S ∈R-alg
[Fo, 4.3.2].
(c) (Centre Z(B)) If the underlying R–module of B is faithfully projective,
then Z(B) is a faithfully projective R–module and hence a finite ´etale R–
algebra [Fo, 4.4.6].
(d) (B–modules) Any exact sequence of B–modules, which is split-exact
as sequence of R–modules, is also split exact as sequence of B–modules. Any
B–module, which is projective as R–module, is also projective as B–module
[Fo, 4.4.1].
(e) (Left ideals) Let L ⊂B be a left ideal such that B/L is projective as
R–module. Then there exists an idempotent c ∈B such that L = Bc and
hence L and B/L ∼= B(1B −c) are projective as B–module.
Indeed, the exact sequence 0 →L →B →B/L →0 of B–modules is
split-exact as exact sequence of R–modules. Hence, by d, it is split-exact
as sequence of B–modules. Thus, there exists a left ideal L′ ⊂B such that
L ⊕L′ = B. Decomposing 1B = c + c′ with c ∈L, c′ ∈L′, one finds that c
is an idempotent with L = Bc and L′ = B(1B −c) [Fo, 1.1.20].
The idempotent c is in general not uniquely determined by L.
(f) (Groups) If B is finite projective as R–module, the R–group scheme
GL1(B), defined in 1.10(g), is reductive [CF, 3.1.0.50].
13.2. Azumaya algebras. Recall that an R–algebra A is an Azumaya R–
algebra if it is a separable R–algebra whose centre Z(A) is isomorphic to
R1A under the map R →Z(A), r 7→r1A, where 1A is the identity element
of A. Some references for Azumaya algebras over rings are [DI], [Fo], [Gr],


## Page 183


QUADRATIC SPACES OVER SEMILOCAL RINGS
183
[Knu, III, §5], [KO, Ch. III and IV] and [Sal]. Azumaya algebras over fields
are for example considered in [B:A3] and [GS].
We review some facts used later. We denote by rankR A the rank of the
faithfully projective R–module underlying the Azumaya R–algebra A and
by degR A the degree of A, defined by (degR A)2 = rankR A. Both are locally
constant functions on Spec(R) with values in N+.
(a) (Base change) Let S ∈R-alg. Then the tensor product algebra AS =
A ⊗R S is an Azumaya S–algebra [Fo, 7.1.9].
(b) (Direct products) Let R = R1×· · ·×Rn be a direct product of rings and
let Ai, i = 1, . . . , n, be Ri–algebras. Then A is a separable (an Azumaya)
R–algebra if and only if every Ai is a separable (an Azumaya) Ri–algebra.
Using the rank decomposition of finite projective R–module, this allows to
reduced questions on general Azumaya algebras to those of constant rank.
(c) (Double Centralizer Theorem [Fo, 7.2.2]) We denote by CA(B) = {a ∈
A : ab = ba for all b ∈B} the centralizer subalgebra of an R–subalgebra
B of A. If B is separable, then so is B′ = CA(B) and CA(B′) = B holds.
Moreover, if B is an Azumaya R–algebra, then so is B′ and the multiplication
of A gives rise to an isomorphism B ⊗R B′
∼
−→A of R–algebras.
(d) (Examples) Let P be a faithfully projective R–module. Then EndR(P)
is an Azumaya R–algebra.
More generally, let Q be a faithfully projective left A–module (called
a “progenerator” in [Fo]).
The restriction P = RA/R(Q) is a faithfully
projective R–module [Fo, 1.1.8], hence EndR(P) is an Azumaya R–algebra.
It contains the R–subalgebra LA = {La : a ∈A}, where La : A →A,
x 7→ax, is the left multiplication of A by a. It is isomorphic to A. By (c),
the centralizer algebra CEndR(P)(LA) = EndA(Q) is an Azumaya R–algebra.
(e) (Tensor products) If A and B are Azumaya R–algebras, the tensor
product algebra A ⊗R B is an Azumaya algebra [Fo, 7.1.3]. In particular,
the matrix algebra Mn(A) = Mn(R)⊗R A, n ≥1, is an Azumaya R–algebra.
(f) (Isomorphisms) Let B be a second Azumaya R–algebra.
Any R–
algebra homomorphism f : A →B is injective; it is an isomorphisms if and
only if rankR A = rankR B [Fo, 7.6.1].
(g) (Neutralizing and splitting rings) We say that A is neutral if A ∼=
EndR(M) for some faithfully projective R–module M. Following [KO, III,
§6] we call S ∈R-alg a neutralizing ring if if there exists a faithfully projec-
tive S–module M and an S–algebra isomorphism α: A ⊗R S
∼
−→EndS(M).
In this case, we will call α a neutralization of A and say A ⊗R S is neutral.
Thus A is neutral if and only if its Brauer class is the neutral element of
Br(R). If S is a neutralizing ring of A that is faithfully flat as R–module, we
will refer to S and α as a faithfully flat neutralizing ring and faithfully flat
neutralization respectively. Every Azumaya algebra A has a faithfully flat
neutralizing ring, even a neutralizing ring that is finite ´etale as R–algebra
[Fo, 10.3.9], [KO, III, 6.6].

We say that A is split if A ∼= Mn(R) for some n ∈N. In this case, A has
constant rank n2. Conversely, if A has constant rank, necessarily of the form
n2 for some n ∈N+, there exists a faithfully flat ´etale S ∈R-alg and an
S–algebra isomorphisms α: A ⊗R S
∼
−→Mn(S) [Fo, 10.3.10], here referred
to as a splitting ring and a splitting.
The reader should be warned that in [Fo, Sal] and several other references
a neutralization as defined above is called a “splitting”. Our terminology
follows the general practice that a split object in a category should be unique
up to isomorphism, which neutralizations need not be.
(h) (Commutative subalgebras) Let S ⊂A be a commutative R–subalgebra.
Viewing A as a right S–module, the sandwich map A ⊗R Aop
∼

a ⊗b 7→LaRb = (x 7→axb) restricts to a unital S–algebra homomorphism
(13.2.1)
αS : A ⊗R S −→EndS(A),
a ⊗s 7→LaRs.
If A is faithfully projective as S–module, αS is injective by (d) and (f).
(i) (Maximal commutative subalgebras) An R–subalgebra S of A is a maxi-
mal commutative subalgebra if it is maximal with respect to inclusion among
the commutative R–subalgebras of A. Maximal commutative subalgebras
exist by Zorn.
(I) A commutative R–subalgebra S of A is maximal commutative if and
only if S = CA(S). Another criterion:
(13.2.2)
S is maximal commutative
⇐⇒
αS is an isomorphism.
If S is maximal commutative, then αS is an isomorphism, see for example
[Fo, 7.4.2] or [KO, III, 6.1]. Conversely, if αS is an isomorphism, then the
centre of EndS(A) is {s IdA : s ∈S} ∼= S since A ⊗R S is an Azumaya
S–algebra by (a). If S′ ⊃S is a commutative subalgebra of A, then Rs′,
s′ ∈S, commutes with all LaRs for a ∈A and s ∈S. Therefore Rs′ = Rs
for some s ∈S, forcing s′ = s ∈S.
We note that even when αS is an isomorphism, it need not be a neutral-
ization since it is not clear that the S–module A is projective. Details of the
following results are given in [Fo, 7.4.2] or [KO, III, 6.1].
(II) Assume that S is maximal commutative and that the S–module A
is projective. It is then faithfully projective, hence αS is a neutralization.
The neutralizing ring S is then even faithfully projective as R–module, in
particular it is a faithfully flat neutralizing ring. Moreover, S is a direct
summand of the S–module A ([KO, III, 1.9]).
Denoting by rankR M : Spec(R) →Z the rank function of a finite projec-
tive R–module M and by φ: Spec(S) →Spec(R) the canonical continuous
map, which is surjective by faithful flatness, the following equalities hold:
(rankR A) ◦φ = (rankS A)2,
(rankR S) ◦φ = rankS A,
rankR A = (rankR S)2.

(l) (Brauer equivalence) Among the various characterizations of Brauer
equivalence ([Fo, §7.3], [KO, III, 5.6], [Sal, Lem. 3.4]) we will use the follow-
ing: an Azumaya R–algebra B is Brauer equivalent to A, in symbols B ∼A,
if one of the following equivalent conditions holds:
(i) A ⊗R EndR(P) ∼= B ⊗R EndR(Q) for some faithfully projective R–
modules P and Q,
(ii) B ∼= EndA(P) for some faithfully projective right A–module P,
(iii) A ⊗R Bop ∼= EndR(M) for some faithfully projective R–module M,
(iv) there exists r ∈N+ and an idempotent c ∈Mr(A) with AnnR(c) =
{0} and B ∼= c Mr(A) c.
The equivalence of (i), (ii) and (iii) is [KO, III, 5.6] (and also [Fo, 7.3.4]).
Given (ii), we choose r ∈N+ such that Ar = P ⊕Q for a right A–module Q.
The projection of Ar onto P is an idempotent c ∈EndA(Ar) = Mr(A) for
which (iv) holds. Retracing the steps (or using [Sal, Lem. 3.4]) shows (iv)
=⇒(ii).
The following Lemma 13.3 clarifies the role of maximal ´etale subalgebras
of an Azumaya R–algebra.


## Page 186


186
P. GILLE AND E. NEHER (JUNE 30, 2026)
13.3. Lemma (´Etale subalgebras). Let S be a commutative R–subalgebra of
the Azumaya R–algebra A.
(a) Then
S is a separable R–algebra
⇐⇒
S is a finite ´etale R–algebra.
In this case,
(i) A is a faithfully projective S–module, hence 13.2(iII) applies, in par-
ticular αS is a faithfully flat neutralization, and
(ii) S is a direct summand of the S–module A and the R–module A.
(b) Let S be a separable, hence finite ´etale R–algebra. Then
(13.3.1)
S is maximal commutative
⇐⇒
degR A = rankR S.
Regarding (13.3.1) we recall that both rankR and degR are considered as
locally constant functions on Spec(R).
Proof. (a) If S is a separable R–algebra, [Fo, 4.4.1(2)] says that A is a
projective S–module. It is then necessarily faithfully projective. It follows
([KO, III, 1.9]) that S is a direct summand of the S–module A and therefore
also a direct summand of the R–module A. As such, S is a finite projective
R–module and therefore a finite projective R–algebra, hence a finite ´etale
R–algebra.
(b) If S is maximal commutative, then degR A = (rankR A)2 = rankR S by
(13.2.3). For the proof of the converse, we note that S is a direct summand
of the R–module A by (ii). Also, it is no harm to assume that rankR S
is constant. Then [Gr, 5.4] says that the S–algebra homomorphism αS of
(13.2.1) is an isomorphism. By (13.2.2), S is maximal commutative.
□
13.4. Definition (Maximal ´etale). Following Grothendieck [Gr, 5.6] we call
a finite ´etale R–subalgebra of A a maximal ´etale R–subalgebra of A, if it
satisfies (13.3.1).
Every Azumaya R–algebra A over a local ring contains a maximal ´etale
subalgebra; one can even assume that it is one-generated [Fo, 10.3.2]. We
will extend this to the semilocal case in 13.7, using the Hamilton-Cayley
Theorem (13.5.3) for Azumaya algebras. We point out that in general A
need not contain a maximal ´etale subalgebra [KO, III, 6.5], although A
always has a finite ´etale neutralizing ring [Fo, 10.3.9].

polynomial. For every a ∈A there exist unique elements NrdA/R(a) ∈R
and TrdA/R(a) ∈R, called reduced norm and reduced trace respectively,
and a unique polynomial PcrdA/R(a; X) ∈R[X], called the reduced char-
acteristic polynomial of a, such that for every faithfully flat neutralization

13.7. Lemma. Let A be an Azumaya algebra over a semilocal ring R. Then
A contains a maximal ´etale subalgebra.
If A has constant rank, then A
contains a maximal ´etale subalgebra which is one-generated.

Proof. Azumaya algebras and ´etale algebras respect direct products, 13.2(b)
and 1.11(h). It is therefore sufficient to prove the second assertion.
Let degR A = r, let κ1, . . . , κn be the residue fields of R, thus R/Jac(R) =
κ1×· · ·×κn. Then A/Jac(R)A = A⊗R(R/Jac(R)) = A1×· · ·×An is a direct
product of Azumaya κi–algebras of degree r. By [Fo, 7.5.7], each Ai contains
a maximal ´etale κi–algebra Si, which is one-generated, say Si = κi[si] for
some si ∈Si. We know that each Si is r–dimensional, for example from
(13.3.1).
Let s ∈A be a lift of (s1, . . . , sn) ∈A/Jac(R)A and let S = R[s] =
SpanR{sn : n ∈N}. By (13.5.4), the reduced characteristic polynomial is
monic of degree r. Since Pcrd(s; s) = 0 by (13.5.3), the R–subalgebra S
is S = SpanR{1A, s, . . . , sr−1}; it is free of rank r by Nakayama 1.12, it is
finite ´etale since every reduction S ⊗κi = κi[si] is finite ´etale, and it is then
maximal ´etale by (13.3.1).
□

´etale”. Because A is faithfully projective as R–module, the T–module A is
faithfully projective by 13.1(d). It then follows from 13.2(h) that αT is an
injective T–algebra homomorphism.
(b) Since T is a finite ´etale R–algebra, S is finite ´etale as T–algebra
because the property “finite ´etale” respects base change. Also, as the tensor
product of two finite ´etale R–algebras, S is a finite ´etale R–algebra.
It is straightforward to check that A becomes an S–module with the S–
action (13.10.2). The restriction of this S–action to R is the given R–action
on A. Hence, by 13.1(d), the S–module A is projective, and then obviously
faithfully projective.
(c) It is immediate that β is a well-defined S–algebra homomorphism.
Since D is an Azumaya Z–algebra, D⊗ZS is an Azumaya S–algebra, 13.2(a).
Also EndS(M) is an Azumaya S–algebra, 13.2(d). Hence β is an injective
S–algebra homomorphism by 13.2(h). The equation (13.10.4) holds by con-
struction.
(d) Since both maps are R–linear, it suffices to prove bijectivity after
localization in a maximal ideal of R, in other words, we can assume that R
is local.

13.17. Epilogue: another approach to reduced gadgets. As noted in
13.5, our approach to the reduced characteristic polynomial, reduced trace
and reduced norm goes back to Grothendieck [Gr]. Another approach is
based on the Hamilton-Cayley Theorem: every matrix satisfies its character-
istic polynomial. This approach, classical for associative algebras over fields,
was pursued by Jacobson [Ja1, Ja2] for finite-dimensional power-associative
algebras over fields, which includes Jordan algebras over fields of character-
istic ̸= 2. That this second approach can also be followed in the setting
of Azumaya algebras over schemes, has been pointed out by Grothendieck
in [Gr, 5.13]. Jacobson’s work has been taken up by Loos in [Lo3], where
he considers Jordan algebras over rings R whose underlying R–modules are
finite projective and which are generically algebraic, thus in particular Azu-
maya algebras. In Loos’ setting, a version of Lemma 13.9 is proven in [Lo3,
2.9].
14. Norm groups and ´etale norm groups
Unless specified otherwise, in this section A denotes an Azumaya R–
algebra, not necessarily of constant rank.
The goal of this section is to study norm groups and ´etale norm groups
of Severi-Brauer schemes and quadrics. We start with an introduction to
Severi-Brauer schemes in 14.1 and to norm groups for arbitrary schemes in
14.2.
14.1. Severi-Brauer schemes. Throughout, A is an Azumaya R–algebra
of degree degR : Spec(R) →Z, thus (degR A)2 = rankR A.
We mainly
follow van den Bergh’s approach [vdB]. We first recall three descriptions of
the R–points of the Severi-Brauer scheme of A.
(a) We consider pairs (P, φ) consisting of a left A–module P, which is finite
projective as R–module and satisfies rankR P = degR A, and a surjective A–
module homomorphism φ: A →P. Two such pairs (P, φ) and (P ′, φ′) are
called equivalent, if there exists an A–module isomorphism u: P
∼
−→P ′
satisfying φ′ = u ◦φ. We denote by [P, φ] the equivalence class of a pair

(b) We mention another description of SB(A). We consider triples (P, θ, x)
consisting of a finite projective R–module P with rankR P = degR A, a
unital R–algebra homomorphism θ: A →EndR(P), and an element x ∈P
satisfying θ(A) Rx = P. Two such triples (P, θ, x) and (P ′, θ′, x′) are called

u(x) = x′) and u ◦θ(a) ◦u−1 = θ′ for all a ∈A. We let SB′(A) be the set of
equivalence classes of triples (P, θ, x) as defined above.

In 14.9 we will determine the norm groups and ´etale norm groups of the
Severi-Brauer scheme SB(A) of an Azumaya R–algebra A for R semilocal,
see 14.1(d) for the definition of SB(A). The following Lemma 14.6 relates
neutrality of an Azumaya algebra, cf. 13.2(g), to points of Severi-Brauer
schemes.
14.6. Lemma. Let X = SB(A) be the Severi-Brauer scheme of the Azumaya
R–algebra A.
(a) If X(R) ̸= ∅, then A is neutral, i.e., A ∼R.
(b) If A ∼R, say A = EndR(M) for a faithfully projective R–module,
then X(R) ̸= ∅⇐⇒M contains a complemented rank-1-submodule.


## Page 202


202
P. GILLE AND E. NEHER (JUNE 30, 2026)
(c) Let R be a unimodular ring, 1.6. Then X(R) ̸= ∅⇐⇒A is neutral.
(d) Let S ⊂A be a maximal commutative R–subalgebra, for which the
canonical S–module A is faithfully projective, e.g., assume that S is maximal
´etale. Then X(S) ̸= ∅.
Proof. (a) If X(R) ̸= ∅, then 14.1(c) shows that A contains a left ideal L such
that A/L is a projective R–module of rank degR(A). The left multiplication
then gives rise to an R–algebra homomorphism A →EndR(A/L), which is
an isomorphism by 13.2(f).
(b) We know from B.15(d) that X(R) ̸= ∅⇐⇒P(M) ̸= ∅.
(c) The implication ⇒follows from (a). Conversely, let A be neutral, i.e.,
A ∼= EndR(M) for some faithfully projective R–module M. By definition
of unimodularity, M contains a unimodular vector, implying X(R) ̸= ∅by
(b).
(d) Let P be the AS–module defined on A by (a ⊗s)(p) = aps. Then
AS →P, a⊗s 7→as, is an epimorphism of AS–modules. Let φ: Spec(S) →
Spec(R) be the canonical map. Then rankS P = (rankR S) ◦φ = (degR A) ◦
φ = degS(A ⊗R S) follows from (13.2.3), so that (P, φ) gives rise to an
element of SB′(AS) ∼= SB(S).
If S ⊂A is maximal ´etale, then the S–
module A is faithfully projective by 13.3(i).
□
Remarks. The implication in 14.6(a) cannot be reversed. Indeed, if A =
EndR(M) and X(R) ̸= ∅, then M is decomposable by (b). But there exist
indecomposable faithfully projective R–modules, for example the tangent
bundle of the 2–dimensional real sphere is such an example ([Lam2, Remark
after I, Prop. 4.15]).

Appendix A. Direct limits, Noetherian reduction
With the exception of the Examples A.8, throughout this appendix we
assume that R = lim
−→Rλ for a given directed system (Rλ)λ∈Λ of R0–rings,
as reviewed in A.1.
A.1. Directed systems and direct limits. In this subsection we review
directed (= inductive) systems and their limits, here called direct limits but
elsewhere sometimes referred to as inductive limits or colimits. We refrain
from expounding a general theory of directed systems in categories, but
rather restrict ourselves to the situation needed here and elsewhere in the
book.
Let (Λ, ≤) be a directed poset, i.e., Λ is a non-empty set with a binary
relation ≤, which is reflexive, transitive, and has the property that for λi ∈
Λ, i = 1, 2, there exists µ ∈Λ satisfying λi ≤µ.
Throughout we fix R0 ∈Z-alg, and a directed system (Rλ, φµλ)λ∈Λ be a
directed system in R0-alg. Thus, by definition, every Rλ is an R0–algebra
and the maps φµλ : Rλ →Rµ, which are assumed to exist for λ ≤µ, are uni-
tal R0–algebra homomorphisms satisfying φλλ = IdRλ and φνµ ◦φµλ = φνλ
whenever λ ≤µ ≤ν. Associated with such a system are an R0–algebra
R = lim
−→Rλ, the direct limit of (Rλ), and R0–algebra homomorphisms
φλ : Rλ →R satisfying φµ ◦φµλ = φλ for λ ≤µ and R = S
λ∈Λ φλ(Rλ).
The R0–algebra R enjoys a universal property, analogous to the one recalled
below for modules. Examples of direct limits are given in A.8.
To facilitate quoting [EGA], we note that the data R0, (Rλ, φµλ)λ∈Λ and
R = lim
−→Rλ give rise to an affine base scheme S0 = Spec(R0), an inverse (=
projective) system (Sλ, uµλ)λ∈Λ = (Spec(Rλ), Spec(φµλ))λ∈Λ of S0–schemes,
and an S0–scheme S = Spec(R) = lim
←−Sλ, the limit of (Sλ, uµλ). By Yoneda,
the converse is also true, i.e., an affine base scheme S0, an inverse system
(Sλ, uµλ) of affine S0–schemes and its limit S = lim
←−Sλ arise from unique
data R0, (Rλ, φµλ) and R as above.
A directed system (Pλ, fµλ) of modules associated with the directed sys-
tem (Rλ) consists of a family of right Rλ–modules Pλ and dimorphisms
fµλ, µ ≥λ, i.e., additive maps fµλ : Pλ →Pµ satisfying fµλ(pλrλ) =
fµλ(pλ)φµλ(rλ) for pλ ∈Pλ and rλ ∈Rλ. Associated with such a system are
an R–module lim
−→Pλ, the direct limit of (Pλ), and dimorphisms fµ : Pµ →
lim
−→Pλ for which fµ ◦fµλ = fλ whenever λ ≤µ and lim
−→Pλ = S
λ∈Λ fλ(Pλ)
holds. The R–module lim
−→Pλ has the following universal property: given
an R–module Q and dimorphisms uλ : Pλ →Q satisfying uµ ◦fµλ = uλ
for µ ≥λ, there exists a unique R–module map u: lim
−→Pλ →Q such that

A.2. Some standard facts. We keep the notation of A.1: (Λ, ≤) is a di-
rected poset and (Rλ, φµλ) is a directed system in R0-alg with R = lim
−→Rλ.
(a) (Reduction of modules) An R–module M is finitely presented if and
only if there exist λ ∈Λ and a finitely presented Rλ–module Mλ such that
M ∼= Mλ ⊗Rλ R ([EGA, IV2, 5.13.7.1], [St, Tag 05N7(1)].
(b) (Reduction of homomorphisms) Let φ: M →N be an R–linear map
between finitely presented R–modules. By (a) we can assume that there
exist λ ∈Λ and finitely presented Rλ–modules Mλ and Nλ such that M =
Mλ ⊗Rλ R and N = Nλ ⊗Rλ R. Then there exist µ ∈Λ, µ ≥λ, and an
Rµ–module map φµ : Mλ ⊗Rλ Rµ →Nλ ⊗Rλ Ru such that φ = φµ ⊗1R, [St,
Tag 05N7(2)].
(c) (Reduction of epi- and isomorphisms) In the setting of (b), suppose
that φ: M →N is a surjective R–linear map or an isomorphism. For µ′ ∈Λ,
µ′ ≥µ, we put Mµ′ = (Mλ ⊗Rλ Rµ) ⊗Rµ Rµ′ = Mλ ⊗Rλ Rµ′ and employ the
analogous notation for N. Then there exists ν ∈Λ, ν ≥µ such that
φν = φ ⊗1Rν : Mν →Nν
is surjective or, respectively, an isomorphism.
Indeed, let xi, i = 1, . . . , m be generators of Mµ. We can pick ν ≥µ such
that Nν contains all φµ(xi ⊗1Rν). Then φµ is surjective. In case φ is an
isomorphism, one applies (b) for φ and φ−1 and uses [St, Tag 05N7(3)] to
conclude.
A.3. Lemma (Reduction of module properties). We continue with the set-
ting of A.1 and A.2:
(Rλ, φµλ)λ∈Λ is a directed system in R0-alg with
R = lim
−→Rλ. Let P be one of the following properties of a module:
(i) flat,
(ii) finite projective,
(iii) projective of rank r,
(iv) faithfully projective.
Let M be a finitely presented R–module. Then M has property P if and
only if there exist λ ∈Λ and an Rλ–module Mλ which has property P and
satisfies M ∼= Mλ ⊗Rλ R.
Proof. Since the properties P are stable under base change, we only have to
prove the existence of Mλ with property P. By A.2(a), there exist λ ∈Λ

and a finitely presented Rλ–module Mλ such that M = Mλ ⊗Rλ R. We thus
get a directed system (Mµ = Mλ ⊗Rλ Rµ)µ≥λ of finitely presented modules.
Then [St, Tag 02JO] with S = R says that there exists µ ≥λ such that
Mµ is flat = finite projective, proving (i) and (ii). A direct proof of (ii) and
(iii) is given in [EGA, IV3, 8.5.5]. Finally, (iv) follows by applying the rank
decomposition of modules.
□
We also need to understand the behaviour of bilinear maps.
A.4. Lemma (Bilinear maps). As before, R = lim
−→Rλ. Let M, N and P
be finitely presented R–modules and let b: M × N →P be an R–bilinear
map. Then there exist λ ∈Λ, finitely presented Rλ–modules Mλ, Nλ and
Pλ and an Rλ–bilinear map bλ : Mλ × Nλ →Pλ such that M = Mλ ⊗Rλ R,
N = Nλ ⊗Rλ R, P = Pλ ⊗Rλ R and b = (bλ)R are obtained by base change.
Special cases of interest are the following:
(i) If M = N, we can assume Mλ = Nλ;
(ii) if M = N = P, we can assume Mλ = Nλ = Pλ, in other words, an
R–algebra (M, b) is obtained by base change from an Rλ–algebra for
suitable λ ∈Λ;
(iii) if P = R, we can assume Pλ = Rλ;
(iv) if M is finite projective or projective of rank r, we can assume that
so is Mλ; analogously for N and P;
(v) if the R–linear map M ⊗R N →P, m ⊗n 7→b(m, n) is bijective,
then we can choose λ, Mλ, Nλ, Pλ and bλ such that
Mλ ⊗Rλ Nλ →Pλ,
mλ ⊗nλ 7→bλ(mλ, nλ),
is bijective.
Proof. By A.2(a) there exist λ ∈Λ and finitely presented Rλ–modules Mλ,
Nλ and Pλ such that M = Mλ ⊗Rλ R, N = Nλ ⊗Rλ R and P = Pλ ⊗Rλ R.
We can then view b as an R–linear map
b: M ⊗R N = (Mλ ⊗Rλ Nλ) ⊗Rλ R −→Pλ ⊗Rλ R.
Since Mλ ⊗Rλ Nλ is a finitely presented Rλ–module, A.2(b) says that there
exists µ ∈Λ, µ ≥λ and an Rµ–linear map
bµ : Mλ ⊗Rλ Nλ ⊗Rλ Rµ −→Pλ ⊗Rλ Rµ
such that b = bµ ⊗Rµ IdR. Taking into account
Mλ ⊗Rλ Nλ ⊗Rλ Rµ ∼= (Mλ ⊗Rλ Rµ) ⊗Rµ (Nλ ⊗Rλ Rµ),
this proves the general claim. The special cases (i)–(iii) hold by the construc-
tion above, (iv) is a consequence of A.3(iii), and (v) follows from A.2(c).
□
A.5. Proposition (Flat cover). Let S ∈R-alg be a flat cover of R = lim
−→Rλ.
Then there exists λ ∈Λ and a flat cover Sλ of Rλ such that S = Sλ ⊗Rλ R.

Proof. Recall that S ∈R-alg is a flat cover of R if and only if S is finitely
presented as R–algebra and faithfully flat as R–module.
By [EGA, IV1,
1.8.4.2] there exists λ ∈Λ and a finitely presented Rλ–algebra such that
S ∼= Sλ ⊗Rλ R. From [EGA, IV3, 11.2.6.1.(ii)] (or [St, Tag 02JO(iii)] with
S = M) we then get that there exists µ ∈Λ, µ ≥λ, such that Sµ =
Sλ ⊗Rλ Rµ is a flat Rµ–module (and a finitely presented Rµ–algebra). More-
over, by [EGA, IV3, 8.10.5(vi)], there exists ν ≥µ such that the morphism
Spec(Sν) →Spec(Rν) is surjective. Thus Sν is faithfully flat as Rν–module.
Since it is also finitely presented by the choice of Sλ, the algebra Sν is a flat
cover.
□

−→Rλ.
(a) Let (M, b) be a regular bilinear R–module in the sense of 4.1. Then

an Rλ–quadratic module (Mλ, qλ) such that (M, q) = (Mλ, qλ)R. If (M, q)
is regular (nonsingular respectively), one can choose λ such that (Mλ, qλ) is
regular (nonsingular respectively).

(b) By [Bae, I, (1.7)] there exists a bilinear form b: M ×M →R such that

projective Rλ–module Mλ and a bilinear form bλ : Mλ ×Mλ →Rλ such that
b = (bλ)R. Define qλ : Mλ →Rλ by qλ(mλ) = bλ(mλ, mλ). Then (Mλ, qλ) is
an Rλ–quadratic module such that (Mλ, qλ)R = (M, q). For µ ≥λ, we let
(Mµ, qµ) = (Mλ, qλ)Rµ; its polar is Rµ–extension of the polar of qλ.
Let (M, q) be regular, i.e., the polar bq of q is regular. Then (a) says that
there exists µ ∈Λ for which bqµ is regular, i.e., qµ is regular.
Finally, let (M, q) be nonsingular. We have seen that there exist λ ∈Λ

decompose (Mλ, qλ) and (M, q) with respect to the rank decomposition of
M and Mλ. Since nonsingularity respects this decomposition, 4.4(i), it is no
harm to assume that both M and Mλ have constant rank. This then also

space (NZ, hZ) over Z and a flat R–cover S such that (M, q)S is isometric
to (NZ, hZ)S = (NS, hS).
By A.5 we can assume that there exists a flat cover Sλ of Rλ such that
S ∼= (Sλ)R.

A.8. Examples of direct limits. (a) Let R ∈Z-alg be arbitrary, and let
Φ(R) be the set of finitely generated unital Z–subalgebras of R, which is a
directed poset with respect to inclusion. It is well-known that
(A.8.1)
R = lim
−→F∈Φ(R) F.
Since every F ∈Φ(R) is a noetherian ring, the reductions in A.3 and A.6
with respect to (A.8.1) are usually referred to as noetherian reduction.
(b) We claim: Every semilocal ring R is a direct limit of semilocalizations
of finitely generated unital Z–subalgebras of R.
Indeed, let m1, . . . , mc be the maximal ideals of R. For F ∈Φ(R) the
ideals pFi = F ∩mi are prime ideals of F. Let F ′ be the semilocalization of F
in pF1, . . . , pFc, see 3.3. If F1 ∈Φ(R) with F ⊂F1, the inclusion canonically
extends to a ring homomorphism F ′ →F ′
1, giving rise to a directed system
(F ′)F∈Φ(R) whose direct limit is isomorphic to the semilocalization of R in
m1, . . . mc, thus to R.

Appendix B. Some concepts and results from algebraic
geometry
We use concepts and results from algebraic geometry which can be found
in [EGA, GW, St]. In this appendix we review some of them, explain our
notation and present some results that we could not find in the quoted
references.
B.1. Some notation, terminology and facts. (a) As in the main body
of the paper, R always denotes a commutative ring, Rr is the localization of
R at r ∈R, Max(R) is the subset of Spec(R) consisting of maximal ideals of
R, R-alg is the category whose objects are commutative associative unital
R–algebras and whose morphisms are unital R–algebra homomorphisms.
An R–functor is a functor from the category R-alg to the category of
sets. We will usually denote R–functors by underlined letters and use the
same letter in bold to denote the scheme representing a given R–functor,
should it exist. An R–scheme is a scheme over Spec(R). For T ∈R-alg
and an R–scheme X we abbreviate XT = X ×R T = X ×Spec(R) Spec(T).
For an affine scheme X = Spec(A), A ∈R-alg, and f ∈A we denote by
Xf = D(f) ∼= Spec(Af) the principal open subscheme determined by f. We
have a canonical isomorphism of T–schemes
(B.1.1)
Xf ×R T ∼= (X ×R T)f⊗1T .
(b) (Some R–schemes) An R–module M defines an R–functor W(M) by
A(∈R-alg) 7→M ⊗R A = MA and given in the obvious way on morphisms
in R-alg. If M is finitely presented, the R–functor W(M) is representable if
and only if M is finite projective [Rom, 5.4.5]. In this case we write W(M)
for the scheme representing W(M); it is given by Spec(S(M ∨)), where M ∨
is the R–dual of M and S(·) is its symmetric R–algebra.
In particular,
W(M) is an affine smooth finitely presented R–scheme with geometrically
connected fibres. For clarity, we sometimes write W(M) = WR(M).
For an R–module M and R′ ∈R-alg we have
(B.1.2)
WR(M) ×R R′ ∼= WR′(M ⊗R R′).
Let B be an R–algebra, whose underlying R–module is finite projective.
The R–functor of automorphisms of B, defined by A 7→Aut(BA), is repre-
sented by an R–scheme, denoted Aut(B), [DG, II, §1, 2.6]. The R–functor
A 7→(B ⊗R A)× is representable by an R–scheme, denoted GL1(B) or
GL1,R(B) if it is helpful to indicate R, [CF, 2.4.2.2].
(c) In this appendix, we mostly consider schemes over a base scheme S,
also called S–schemes. For an S–scheme X and x ∈X, we denote κ(x) =
OX,x /mx where OX,x is the local ring of X at x and mx its maximal ideal.
For S-schemes X and Y we put X(Y ) = MorS(Y, X), the S–morphisms
from Y to X, and abbreviate X(Spec(A)) = X(A) for any affine scheme
Y = Spec(A).

(d) A scheme X is connected (irreducible respectively) if its underlying
topological space is connected (irreducible respectively). It is reduced if all
local rings are reduced (= no non-zero nilpotent elements); it is integral if
it is reduced and irreducible. An affine scheme X = Spec(A) is reduced
(integral) if and only if A is a reduced ring (an integral domain), [St, Tag
01J2] for “reduced”.
Let P be one of the following properties of a scheme over a field: “irre-
ducible”, “connected”, “reduced”, “integral”. One says that a scheme X
over a field k has property P geometrically if for all fields F ∈k-alg the
F–scheme XF = X ×k F has property P. Let K be an algebraically closed
extension of k. Then the k–scheme X has property P geometrically if and
only if the K–scheme XK has property P ([EGA, IV2, (4.5.2) and (4.6.5)]
or [GW, Cor. 5.54]).
(e) (Essentially free morphisms) A morphism f : X →S is essentially free
if there exists an open affine covering (Si)i∈I of S, for every Si an affine and
faithfully flat scheme Si–scheme S′
i = Spec(R′
i) and an open affine covering

of X ×S S′
i such that every Aij is a free R′
i–module. See [SGA3,
VIB, D´ef. 6.2.1], or [GW, Exc. 14.20] where this type of morphism is called
“locally free”.
(f) (Smooth schemes over a field) Let k be a field and let X be a smooth
k–scheme. Then X is locally of finite type, geometrically reduced and geo-
metrically regular in the sense of (d), see [St, Tag 056T] for the last two
properties. Any smooth scheme is locally of finite presentation and so in
particular locally of finite type if the base is a field.
(g) (Schemes locally of finite presentation) An S–scheme X is locally of
finite presentation if and only if for every directed set I and every projective
system (Ti)i∈I of affine schemes the canonical map
lim
−→i∈I MorS(Ti, X)
∼
−→MorS(lim
←−Ti, X)
is a bijection ([EGA, IV3, 8.14.2], see also [St, Tag 01ZC]).
(h) (Reducedness criterion) Let R be an integral domain with fraction
field K. If X is a flat R–scheme and XK is a smooth k–scheme, then X is
reduced.
Indeed, reducedness being a local property, we can assume that X =
Spec(A) for a flat R–algebra A [St, Tag 01U5]. By (f), all local rings of the
K–scheme XK = Spec(A⊗RK) are reduced. Hence, by (d), the ring A⊗RK
is reduced. Flatness of the R–module A implies that A = A ⊗R R embeds
into A ⊗R K. Therefore A is reduced and then X = Spec(A) is reduced by
(d) again.
A particularly interesting case where (h) applies is that of a smooth
scheme X over the integral domain R. Then X is flat and XK is smooth by
[St, Tags 01VF and 01VB]. Hence X is reduced.
(i) (Descent of smoothness) Let X be an R–scheme, locally of finite pre-
sentation and let S ∈R-alg be faithfully flat. Then the S–scheme XS is

(k) (Fibrewise Isomorphism (Open Immersion) Criterion [EGA, IV4, 17.9.5]
or [Con1, Exc. 3.4.3]) Let S be a scheme and let f : X →Y be a morphism
between S–schemes X and Y which are locally of finite presentation. If,
moreover, X is a flat S–scheme, the following are equivalent:
(α) f is an isomorphism (an open immersion respectively);
(β) for every s ∈S the induced map fs : X ×S κ(s) →Y ×S κ(s) is an
isomorphism) of schemes (an open immersion respectively);
(γ) for every s ∈S the induced map f¯s : X ×S κ(s) →S ×S κ(s) is an
isomorphism of κ(s) (an open immersion respectively), where κ(s)
is an algebraic closure of κ(s).
The equivalence of (α) and (β) is [EGA, loc. cit.]. The equivalence of (β)
and (γ) follows by faithfully flat descent ([EGA, IV2, 2.7.1(viii)]).
The following lemma is folklore.
B.2. Lemma. Let X be a regular scheme. Then the following are equivalent:
(i) X is integral;
(ii) X is irreducible;
(iii) X is connected.
If X is a regular scheme over a field, e.g., a smooth scheme, then the geo-
metric version of (i)–(iii) are equivalent:
(i)′ X is geometrically integral;
(ii)′ X is geometrically irreducible;
(iii)′ X is geometrically connected.
Proof. The implications (i)
=⇒
(ii)
=⇒
(iii) hold for any scheme. A
connected regular scheme is integral by [Po, 3.5.6]. The equivalence of the
geometric properties in the field case then follows. Any smooth scheme over
a field is geometrically regular by [St, Tag 056T].
□

the largest closed subscheme S0 of S such that the restrictions f|S0 and g|S0
coincide. Note that there is a priori no reason for the existence of such a
largest closed subscheme.
Furthermore, by [SGA3, loc. cit.], if Z is locally of finite type, then
Ker(f, g) is a finitely presented S–scheme.

(a) We say that a subset A of the topological space underlying X is Zariski
dense or simply is dense if it is so as topological space.
Standard example: A open non-empty subscheme of an irreducible scheme
is dense.
(b) (Zariski density of rational points over fields) Let k be a field. Follow-
ing [Po, 2.1.1], a variety over k or a k–variety is a separated scheme X of
finite type over S = Spec(k). We will add adjectives such as “irreducible”
for more restrictive classes of varieties. For example, a “variety” in the sense
of [St, Tag 020D] is an integral k–variety in our sense. Also recall that a
geometrically integral k–variety X is rational (unirational), if there exists a
rational map from an affine k-space An
k →X, which is birational (dominant
respectively). In particular, any open subscheme of an affine k–space is ra-
tional and a fortiori unirational. We will use the following (of the many)
denseness criteria for rational points.
Given a field extension F/k and a non-empty k–scheme X which is locally
of finite type, the set X(F) of F–rational points is Zariski dense in X in any
one of the following three cases:
(i) F is algebraically closed;
(ii) F is separably closed and X is a geometrically reduced k–variety;
(iii) k = F is infinite and X is a unirational k–variety.
In particular, in any of these cases, if X ̸= ∅, then X(k) is infinite.
For (i) and (ii) we can assume that k = F after base change. Then (i)
and (ii) are proven in [GW, Cor. 3.36] and [Po, Cor. 3.5.71] respectively or
see [Sp2, 11.2.5] for X irreducible. A proof of (iii) is given in [Bo, AG 13.7]
and again in [Sp2, 13.2.6].
(c) (S-density) Slightly generalizing [BLR, §2.5], we say that an open
subscheme U of X is S-dense in X if for every s ∈S the fiber Us = U ×Sκ(s)
is Zariski dense in Xs = X ×S κ(s). If S = Spec(R), we use the term R–
dense instead of S–dense. It is in this sense that we have used the term in
our paper [GN2].
Of course, for schemes over a field R, Zariski dense equals R–dense. But
this is not so for arbitrary R: an R–dense open subscheme of a smooth
scheme is dense, but a dense open subscheme is not necessarily R–dense.
For example, let R be a DVR with fraction field K and residue field κ. The
open subscheme U = Spec(K) of X = Spec(R) is dense, since Spec(K) is
the generic point of X, but U is not R–dense because K ⊗R κ = 0 and so
Uκ = ∅.
B.7. Schematic denseness. We follow the definition of a schematically
dominant morphism Z →X of schemes and a schematically dense subscheme
Z of a scheme X given in [EGA-I, I, (5.4.2)], which is a special case of that
of [EGA, IV3, (11.10.2)] and [SGA3, IX, D´ef. 4.1] where a morphism is
replaced by a family of morphisms. Thus, f : Z →X is a schematically
dominant morphism if and only if

(ii) for every open subset V ⊂X the homomorphism f♭(V ): Γ(V, OX) →
Γ(f−1(V ), OZ) is injective.
A subscheme j : Z →X is called schematically dense if j is schematically
dominant.
The definition above is the same as that of [GW, 9.18, 9.19]. The reader
should be warned that Remark 9.20(1) of the published version of the book
[GW] is not correct, see the book’s errata at www.algebraic-geometry.de.
The correct statement is given in (a) below.
In [St, Tag 01RB], an open subscheme U of a scheme X is called scheme
theoretically dense if
(iii) for every open subscheme V ⊂X the scheme theoretic closure of
U ∩V is V .
That (iii) is equivalent to (i), follows from [St, Tag 01RE].
(a) (Schematically dense versus Zariski dense) Let f : Z →X be a mor-
phism of schemes. If f is schematically dominant, it is dominant in the sense
that |f(Z)| is a dense subset of the topological space |X|. Conversely, if X
is reduced and f is dominant, then f is schematically dominant. Thus, for
a reduced X, dominant = schematically dominant, see [EGA-I, I, (5.4.3)],
or [EGA, IV3, (11.10.4)].
(b) Let f : Z →X be a morphism of S–schemes. Then f is called univer-
sally schematically dominant relative to S if for every base change S′ →S
the corresponding morphism f′ : Z′ = Z ×S S′ →X′ = X ×S S′ is schemat-
ically dominant. One calls an immersion Z →X or even just Z universally
schematically dense relative to S if it is universally schematically dominant
relative to S, [EGA, IV3, (11.10.8)]. We will abbreviate universally schemat-
ically dense relative to S by universally schematically dense if S is clear from
the context or unimportant.
Example: If S = Spec(k), k a field, then schematically dominant = uni-
versally schematically dominant, [EGA, IV3, (11.10.6)].
(c) ([EGA, IV3, (11.10.10)]) Let X →S be flat and locally of finite
presentation, and let U be an open subscheme of X. Then U is univer-
sally schematically dense relative to S if and only if for every s ∈S the
open subscheme Us = U ∩Xs of the Spec(κ(s))–scheme Xs is schematically
dense, equivalently, by (a), every Us is universally schematically dense in
Xs. Hence, since schematically dense implies Zariski dense by (a), we have
the implications
U is universally schematically dense relative to S
=⇒U is S–dense
(B.7.1)
for S–dense in the sense of B.6(c).
(d) Suppose X →S is flat, locally of finite presentation and all fibres Xs,
s ∈S, are reduced. Then, by combining (a) and (c), for any open subscheme

Then there exists an fppf covering S′ →S for which

In the following Lemma B.8 we will consider other situations in which
U(Y ) ̸= ∅, namely Y = Spec(F) for certain S–fields F. Recall that a field
F is an S–field if there exists a morphism of schemes Spec(F) →S.
B.8. Lemma. Let X be an S–scheme which is flat, separated, finitely pre-
sented and has non-empty geometrically reduced fibres. Moreover, let U be
an open subscheme of X.
(a) Then the conditions (i)–(iii) below are equivalent.
(i) U is universally schematically dense in X;
(ii) U(F) ̸= ∅for all separably closed S–fields F;
(iii) U(K) ̸= ∅for all algebraically closed S–fields K.
(b) Suppose in addition that X has geometrically integral fibres and that
X →S is faithfully flat. Then the conditions (i)–(iii) of (a) are equivalent
to
(iv) there exists an fppf covering S′ →S for which U(S′) ̸= ∅.
Proof. (a) (i) =⇒(ii): Since F is an S–field, there exists a unique s ∈S
such that the structure morphism Spec(F) →S factors via the canonical
morphism Spec(k) →S where k is the residue field at s. By B.7(a), we
know that the fibre Uk is Zariski dense in Xk. Since the latter k–scheme is
not empty by assumption, so is Uk. By base change, Xk and then also Uk
are separated, geometrically reduced and finite type k–schemes. The claim
then follows from (ii) of B.6(b).
The implication (ii) =⇒(iii) being trivial, it remains to prove that (iii)
=⇒
(i). Applying B.7(c), it suffices to show that all fibres Us ⊂Xs are
schematically dense. Thus, without loss of generality, we can suppose that
S = Spec(k) for a field k. Let K be an algebraically closed extension field of
k. Then UK is an open non-empty subscheme of the irreducible K–scheme
XK, and is therefore Zariski dense. But XK is also reduced. Hence, UK is
