schematically dense by B.7(a). By [EGA, IV3, Thm. 11.10.5(i)], schemati-
cally dense allows faithfully flat descent. Thus, U is schematically dense, as
required.
(b) The implication (i) =⇒(iv) is [SGA3, XVIII, Prop. 1.7], restated
as B.7(f).
For the converse, let S′ →S be an fppf covering as in (iv).
As mentioned above, the property “universally schematically dense” allows
flat descent.
It is therefore sufficient to prove that the open subscheme
U′ = U ×S S′ of X′ = X ×S S′ is universally schematically dense in X′.
The assumption U(S′) ̸= ∅implies U′(K′) ̸= ∅for some algebraically closed
S′-field K′, so that U′ is universally schematically dense by (a).

B.9. Corollary (Example: Principal open subschemes of W(M)). Let M
be a finitely generated projective R–module, let f ∈S(M ∨) be a polynomial
and let Cont(f) be its content ideal defined in B.5(b). Then the following
are equivalent:
(i) The principal open subscheme W(M)f of W(M) = Spec
 S(M ∨)

is universally schematically dense.
(ii) W(M)f(F) ̸= ∅for all separably closed extension fields F of R.
(iii) W(M)f(K) ̸= ∅for all algebraically closed extension fields K of R.
(iv) Cont(f) = R.
(v) fκ ̸= 0 for every residue field κ = R/m, m a maximal ideal of R.
Proof. Since X = W(M) is smooth with geometrically integral fibres and
U = W(M)f is a principal open subscheme, the equivalence of (i), (ii) and
(iii) is a special case of B.8(a).
If (i) holds, then (W(M)f)κ = W(Mκ)fκ is dense in W(Mκ) for every
residue field κ = R/m, and therefore necessarily (v) is fulfilled. In turn, (v)
implies Cont(f)κ = Cont(fκ) = κ, consequently (iv) follows. Finally, if (iv)
holds, then Cont(f)T = T for every T ∈R-alg. In particular, fK ̸= 0 for
K as in (iii). But then W(MK)fK is well-known to be dense in W(MK), in
particular we have (iii).
□

Even if X′ is representable by an S′–scheme, the S–functor RS′/S(X′)
need not be representable, see (f) below. However, if it is representable, the
representing scheme will also be denoted RS′/S(X′).
We will use the following results, assuming that h: S′ →S is finite locally
free, which in the affine case just means that R′ is a finite projective R–
algebra.
(a) (Existence) If X′ is a quasi-projective S′–scheme, then RS′/S(X′) is
representable by an S–scheme [BLR, 7.6/4].
In particular, RS′/S(X′) is
representable if X′ is a quasi-affine scheme of finite type.
(b) (Inheritance of scheme properties) Let X′ be an S′–scheme for which
RS′/S(X′) exists as an S–scheme. If X′ has one of the properties below,
then the S–scheme RS′/S(X′) has the same property:
(i) finitely presented,
(ii) affine,
(iii) locally of finite type (locally of finite presentation),
(iv) smooth.
(v) quasi-projective, assuming that R and R′ are noetherian.
Indeed, (bii) is established in the proof of [BLR, 7.6/4], for (bi), (biii) and
(biv) see [BLR, 7.6/5], and (bv) is established in [CGP, A.5.8].
(c) (Inheritance of geometrically integral fibres) Let k be a field, let R′
be a finite non-zero k–algebra, and let X′ be a smooth, quasi-projective
R′–scheme whose structure map X′ →Spec(R′) is surjective.
If X′ has
geometrically connected fibres, then RR′/k(X′) is a smooth, non-empty, and
geometrically integral k–scheme.
Indeed, by (biv) and (bv), X = RR′/k(X′) is a smooth quasi-projective k–
scheme, and by [CGP, A.5.9] it is non-empty and geometrically connected.
That X is then geometrically integral, is a consequence of Lemma B.2.
(d) (Quasi-compact open immersions) Let i′ : X′ →Y ′ be an open im-
mersion of finitely presented S′–schemes. Suppose that the Weil restrictions
RS′/S(X′) and RS′/S(Y ′) exist. Then RS′/S(i′): RS′/S(X′) →RS′/S(Y ′) is
an open immersion, which is quasi-compact, equivalently, is of finite presen-
tation.
Indeed, RS′/S(i): RS′/S(X′) →RS′/S(Y ′) is an open immersion by [BLR,
7.6/2]. The R–schemes RR′/R(X′) and RR′/R(Y ′) are finitely presented by
(bi). Hence RS′/S(i) is a finitely presented morphism by [St, Tag 02FV(2)].
An open immersion is of finite presentation if and only if it is quasi-compact
[St, Tag 01TU].

Appendix C. Some results on group schemes
Unless specified otherwise, we consider group schemes over an arbitrary
scheme S.
C.1. Some known results. (a) (Finite presentation of stabilizers) Let G
be an S–group scheme acting on an S-scheme X. As in [SGA3, V, 10.2] let
H/X be the stabilizer of the diagonal ∆: X →X ×S X for the action of
G ×S X on X ×S X, i.e., defined by the right cartesian square in (C.1.1)
where d0 is the action of G on X and d1 is the projection onto the second
factor. For any x ∈X(S) we define the stabilizer Gx of x by the left cartesian
square in (C.1.1):

If G →S and X →S are locally of finite presentation (of finite presentation
respectively), the same holds for the morphisms H →X and Gx →S in the
diagram (C.1.1).
Indeed, both properties allow base change and composition [St, Tags
01TR, 01TS]. Hence, the morphisms G ×S X →S and X ×S S →S are
locally of finite presentation (of finite presentation respectively). But then
so is G ×S X →X ×S X by cancellation [St, Tag 02FV], and then H →X
and Gx →S have these properties, again by base change.
(b) (Smoothness of group schemes over a reduced base) Let X be a scheme
and let H be an X–group scheme. Suppose
(I) X is reduced, e.g. X = Spec(R) for a reduced ring R [St, 01J2], and
(II) H is locally of finite presentation over X, and the geometric fibers
of H are smooth connected and have the same dimension.
Then H is smooth, separated and finitely presented ([SGA3, VIB, 4.4 and
5.5]). See B.1(h) for a criterion establishing the condition (I).
(c) (Smoothness of stabilizers) In the setting of (a), i.e., S is a scheme and
G is an S–group scheme acting on an S–scheme X, assume that X and the
stabilizer H satisfy the conditions (I) and (II) of (b). Then H is smooth,
separated, and of finite presentation over X, and the analogous properties
hold for the S–group scheme Gx for any x ∈X(S).
Indeed, the properties of H follow from the criterion (b), and they imply
the properties of Gx mentioned above, since they are stable under base
change.
In C.2–C.4 we will consider GIT quotients.

The morphism αS′/S is an isomorphism if S′ →S is flat [Ses, Lem. 2],

We say that the GIT quotient X // G is universal if its construction com-
mutes with arbitrary base change, that is, αS′/S is an isomorphism for any

C.3. Lemma (Properties of universal GIT quotients). In the setting of C.2
the following hold.
(a) The universality property for X // G is stable under arbitrary base
change S′ →S.
(b) The universality property of X // G is local for the fpqc topology, as
for example defined in [St, Tag 022B].
(c) The following are equivalent:
(i) X // G is universal;
(ii) For each morphism Spec(R) →S and each R–algebra R′, the
morphism αR′/R : XR′ // GR′ →(XR // GR) ×Spec(R) Spec(R′) is
an isomorphism.
Proof. (a) Assume that X // G is universal and let S′ →S be a morphism
of schemes. To show that (X ×S S′) // (G ×S S′) is universal, let S′′ →S′
be a second scheme morphism. By C.2(b),
(C.3.1)
(αS′/S × IdS′′) ◦αS′′/S′ ◦¯a = aX//G ◦αS′′/S

are isomorphisms by universality of X // G. Since then αS′/S × IdS′′ is an
isomorphism, αS′′/S′ is an isomorphism too, establishing our claim.
(b) Let (Si)i∈I be an fpqc cover of S, put Xi = X ×S Si and Gi = G×S Si
for each i ∈I, and assume that the GIT quotients Xi // Gi are universal.
To show that then X // G is universal too, let T be an S–scheme. We know

the formula (C.2.3) with S′ and T ′ replaced by Si and Ti respectively:
(αSi/S × IdTi) ◦αTi/Si ◦¯aSi = (αT/S × IdTi) ◦αTi/T ◦¯aT .

is an isomorphism for each i ∈I. Since the property of being an isomorphism

that X // G is universal.
(c) The implication (i) =⇒(ii) follows from (a). For the other direction,
we choose a Zariski cover of S by affine schemes, which by [St, Tag 022C]
is a fortiori an fpqc cover of S. Hence, to show that X // G is universal, we
can by (b) assume that S = Spec(A) is an affine scheme.
Let T be an S–scheme and let (Ti)i∈I be a Zariski cover of T by affine
schemes Ti = Spec(Bi). The assumption (ii) implies that each morphism
αBi/A : XBi // GBi →(X // G) ×Spec(A) Spec(Bi) is an isomorphism.
In
other words, the T–morphism XT // GT →(X // G) ×Spec(A) T becomes an
isomorphism after base change to the Zariski cover (Ti)i∈I of T. Applying
again that a Zariski cover is an fpqc cover and [St, Tag 02L4], it follows
that XT // GT →(X // G) ×Spec(A) T is an isomorphism. Thus X // G is
universal.
□
We can now slightly extend Lee’s theorem [Lee] on (Chevalley) adjoint
quotients of reductive group schemes from an affine to an arbitrary base.
See C.7 for a review of reductive group schemes.
C.4. Theorem. Let G be a reductive S–group scheme and consider its action
on itself by inner automorphisms.
(a) The adjoint quotient G // G is universal.
(b) Let T be a maximal S–torus of G and denote by W = NG(T)/T its
Weyl group. Then the map T // W →G // G is an isomorphism.
Proof. (a) In the framework of affine schemes, this is has been proved in
[Lee, Thm. 4.1(1)], so that Lemma C.3(c), (ii) =⇒(i) shows that G // G is
universal in general.

233
(b) According to [St, Tag 02L4], the statement “T // W →G // G is an
isomorphism” is local for the fpqc topology. We can therefore assume that
S is affine and that G (resp. T) is a split reductive S–group (resp. a split
S–torus). In this case, (b) has been established in [Lee, Thm. 4.1(2)].
□
C.5. Identity component of a group scheme. For the notion of the
identity component G0 of a group scheme G over a scheme S, the reader
is referred to [SGA3, VIB, §3], see also [DG, II, §5.1] for S = Spec(k), k a
field. Below we summarize what we will use.
(a) Let k be a field, and let G be a k–group scheme locally of finite
presentation, i.e., a locally algebraic k–group scheme in the terminology of
[DG]. We denote by G0 the open subscheme whose underlying topological
space is the connected component of the topological space |G| containing
e ∈G(k). One knows that G0 is a characteristic k–subgroup scheme of G,
and a closed and open k–subscheme of G.
(b) In the rest of this subsection, let S be a general scheme, let G be an
S–group scheme locally of finite presentation, and let G0 be the S–functor
defined in [SGA3, VIB, §3]. The following is known.
(i) The functor G0 commutes with base change, [SGA3, VIB, 3.3].
(ii) If S is the spectrum of a field, then, by definition, G0 is representable
by the identity component of the locally algebraic group G.
(iii) If G has geometrically connected fibers, then, by definition, G rep-
resents G0.
(iv) If G0 is representable by a scheme G0, the canonical morphism G0 →
G is an open immersion, in particular G0 is an open subgroup scheme
of G [SGA3, VIB, 3.9]. Hence, by (B.3.1), for every affine scheme
Spec(A) over S we have
G0(A) = {g ∈G(A) : gA/m ∈G0(A/m)
for all maximal m ∈Spec(A)}.
(C.5.1)
(v) If G is smooth, then G0 is representable by an open smooth subgroup
scheme of G, denoted G0 [SGA3, VIB, 3.10].
(vi) If G is a finitely presented smooth affine S–group scheme for which
G0
¯s is reductive for all s ∈Spec(R), then G0 is a reductive S–group
scheme that is both open and closed in G, and in particular affine
([Con1, Prop. 3.1.3]).
Warning: Even if G is assumed to be smooth affine, it is in general not
true that G0 is a closed subscheme, nor that it is affine ([SGA3, XIX, 5.13],
[Ra1, VII, §3, (iii)]).
Example G = µ2,R: If 2 ∈R×, then G is smooth, hence G0 is repre-
sentable. If R is a field of characteristic 2, then G0 is not representable by
a scheme.

C.6. Lemma. Let G be an S–group scheme locally of finite presentation and
assume that the S–functor G0 is representable by an S–subgroup scheme G0,
necessarily open by C.5(iv), for example, by C.5(v), assume that G is smooth.
(a) Let f : H →G be a morphism of S–group schemes where H is locally
of finite presentation and has geometrically connected fibers. Then f factors
through G0.
(b) The S–group scheme G0 is the unique open subgroup scheme of G
such that for each s ∈S, G0 ×S κ(s) is the identity component of the locally
algebraic κ(s)-group Gκ(s) = G ×S κ(s).
Proof. (a) We start with the case that S = Spec(k), k a field. Then H is a
connected locally algebraic group and f : H →G is a morphism of locally
algebraic groups. In this case, f−1(G0) is a closed and open k–subgroup
scheme of H by C.5(a). By C.5(iii), H0 is representable by H. It follows
that H = H0 ⊆f−1(G0) ⊆H. Hence f factorizes through G0. The general
case then follows readily from the definition of G0.
(b) By C.5(iii), (G0)s = (Gs)0 for each s ∈S. Let G′ be an open S–
subgroup scheme of G such that (G′)s = (Gs)0. Then (a) shows that the
open immersion i : G′ →G factors through G0.
We thus get an open
immersion i′ : G′ →G0 of S–schemes. Since i′
s is an isomorphism for each
s ∈S, i′ is a surjective open immersion and therefore an isomorphism.
□
C.7. Parabolic subgroups of reductive group schemes. For the con-
venience of the reader, we summarize here the results on parabolic subgroups
of reductive groups over a scheme S that we will use. We follow the termi-
nology and notation of [SGA3], which coincides with the one used in [Con1].
Although in the main part of the text we will only be interested in reductive
groups over an affine base, specializing here to the affine case does not lead
to a great simplification.
(a) An S–group scheme G is called reductive (semisimple respectively) if
it is affine, smooth and all its geometric fibres G¯s, s ∈S, are connected re-
ductive groups (semisimple groups respectively) [Con1, 3.1.1], [SGA3, XIX,
2.7]. In the same vein, a parabolic subgroup of a reductive R–group G is a
smooth subgroup P of G such that all its geometric fibres P¯s, s ∈S, are
parabolic subgroups of the reductive group G¯s, [Con1, 5.2.1], [SGA3, XXVI,
1.1 and 1.2]. In the following, P denotes a parabolic subgroup of a reductive
group G.
(b) The canonical inclusion P →G is a closed immersion. The quotient
sheaf G/P in the ´etale topology on S–schemes is represented by a smooth
projective S–scheme [Con1, 5.2.3], [SGA3, XXII, 5.8.5].
(c) (Unipotent radical) The parabolic subgroup P contains a unique smooth
closed normal subgroup radu(P), the unipotent radical of P, whose geomet-
ric fibres (radu(P))¯s, s ∈S, coincide with the unipotent radical radu(P¯s) of
the parabolic subgroup P¯s of the reductive group G¯s [Con1, 5.2.5], [SGA3,

2.5].
(d) (Levi subgroups) A Levi subgroup is a smooth closed subgroup L ⊂

C.8. Transitivity on the big affine Zariski site. Let S be a scheme
and let G be an S–group scheme acting on an S–scheme X. We say that
the action of G on X is transitive on the big affine Zariski site of S if the
canonical map
G ×S X →X ×S X,
(g, x) 7→(g · x, x)
is an epimorphism of sheaves on the big affine Zariski site of S. Recall that
this means that for every affine scheme U = Spec(A) over S and for every
pair x, y ∈X(U) there exists a standard Zariski cover (a1, . . . , an) of A
such that for every i, 1 ≤i ≤n, there exists gi ∈G(Spec(Aai)) satisfying
gi · xi = yi where xi = x ◦
 Spec(Aai) →Spec(A)

and where yi is defined
analogously.
C.9. Transitivity on the small affine Zariski site. Let S be a scheme
and let G be an S–group scheme acting on an S–scheme X. Replacing the
big affine Zariski site of S by the small affine Zariski site in C.8, we get the
concept of a transitive action of G on X on the small affine Zariski site of S:
for every open immersion U = Spec(A) →S of an affine scheme U and for
every pair x, y ∈X(U) there exists a standard Zariski cover (a1, . . . , an) of
A such that for every i, 1 ≤i ≤n, there exists gi ∈G(Spec(Aai)) satisfying
gi · xi = yi with xi and yi as in C.8.

A.1, A.2] and partially generalized in [GN2].
C.10. Theorem (Demazure’s Conjugacy Theorem [SGA3, XXVI, §3]).
Let G be a reductive R–group scheme, and let X = Par(G)t be the R–scheme
of parabolic subgroups of type t.
(a) Given parabolic subgroups x, y in X(R), there exist f1, . . . , fn ∈R
satisfying f1 +· · ·+fn = 1 and such that yRfi ∈G(Rfi) . xRfi for i = 1 . . . n.
In other words, the action of G on X is transitive on the small affine Zariski
site of Spec(R).
(b) If R is an LG ring, then G(R) acts transitively on X(R).
(c) Assume that R is semilocal and that R′ is a finite R-algebra such that
X(R′) ̸= ∅. Let Jac(R) be the Jacobson radical of R. Then the map
X(R′) −→X(R′/Jac(R)R′) ∼= Q
m∈Spec(R) maximal X(R′/mR′)
is onto.

thus assume that X(R) ̸= ∅and pick a point x ∈X(R), i.e., a parabolic
subgroup P of G of type t, and a parabolic subgroup opposite to P, C.7(e).
Then the claim follows from [GN2, Thm. 4.1(a)] and (C.7.3).

admits a parabolic subgroup Q of type t. As noted in the proof of (b), it

C.12. Reductive group schemes: isotropic versus reducible. Let G
be a reductive group scheme over a scheme S. Following [Gi2], see also [GN2,
5.1], we call G isotropic, if G admits a subgroup isomorphic to Gm,S. We
say that G is reducible if G admits an everywhere proper parabolic subgroup
P, i.e., P¯s ⊊G¯s for all s ∈S and P admits a Levi subgroup. We exhibit
some known results:
(a) ([Gi2, Thm. 7.3.1(2)]) If S is connected (4), a reductive S–group scheme
G is isotropic if and only if G is reducible or the central torus rad(G) is
isotropic. In particular, a semisimple S–group scheme is isotropic if and
only if it is reducible.
(b) ([Gi2, Cor. 7.3.2(2)]) If S is an affine scheme, a reductive S–group
scheme G is isotropic if and only if G admits an everywhere proper parabolic
subgroup or the central torus rad(G) is isotropic.

§4]) Let S = Spec(R) and let G be an R–group scheme. We denote by Gm,R
the multiplicative group over R. A character is a homomorphism χ: G →
Gm,R of R-group schemes, sometimes also called a rational character. The
set of characters is in an obvious way a commutative group, denoted bG(R).

Appendix D. Cohomology
D.1. Pointed sets. We review the basic terminology and some easy results
regarding pointed sets, see for example [KMRT, §28].
A pointed set is a set with a distinguished element, usually denoted (A, ∗A)
where A is a set and ∗A is the distinguished element. For simpler notation
we will abbreviate A = (A, ∗A). We view {∗} as an obviously pointed set.
A morphism f : A →B of pointed sets is a set map f : A →B satisfying
f(∗A) = ∗B. The kernel Ker(f) of a morphism f : A →B of pointed sets is
Ker(f) = {a ∈A : f(a) = ∗B}. A sequence of morphisms of pointed sets
A
f
−→B
β
−→C
is exact if Ker(β) = Im(f) where Im(f) = {f(a) : a ∈A} is the set-theoretic
image. Observe that A
f−→B →{∗} is exact if and only if f is a surjective
set map, while {∗} →A
f−→B is exact if Ker(f) = {∗A}, which does not
imply that f is an injective set map. Obviously, a morphism f : A →B of
pointed sets, whose underlying set map is injective, has trivial kernel.

a morphism of A′-sheaf torsors q−1(b) ∧A A′ →q′−1(b′).
Recall that q−1(b) ∧A A′ is the quotient sheaf (q−1(b) ×S A′)/A where
A acts on q−1(b) ×S A′ by (x, a′) · a = (x · a, f1(a−1)a′) with x ∈q−1(b),
a′ ∈A′(S) and a ∈A(S).
We have a map eΦ: q−1(b) ×S A′ →q′−1(b′)
of sheaves over S, defined on the S–points by eΦ(x, a′) = f(x)a′, which is
constant on the A–orbits and therefore descends to a well-defined sheaf map
Φ: q−1(b) ∧A A′ →q′−1(b′). We let A′ act on q−1(b) ×S A′ by (x, a′) · a′
1.
Since eΦ is equivariant under this A′–action, it follows that Φ is equivariant
too, i.e., Φ is a morphism of A′–torsors.
□

replacing (G, B, B′) there by ( eG, G, G) here. Similarly, replacing (G, A′, B′)
in Lemma D.3 by (A′, eG, B) proves the commutativity of the square (III).
(b) We are given g′ ∈G′(S) with images g = q(g′) ∈G(S) and b = p′(g′) ∈
B(S). By definition φ(g) is the class of the sheaf A–torsor q−1(g) ⊂eG.
